# Grease the Groove Method

The Grease the Groove (GTG) method was developed and popularized by Pavel Tsatsouline in *The Naked Warrior* (2003) and is the most direct practical application of the principle that **neurological skill acquisition is separated from muscular fatigue**. The method's premise: if you want to get better at a movement, the most effective strategy is to practice that movement repeatedly in a non-fatigued state rather than accumulating fatigue across long training sessions.

The phrase "grease the groove" refers to the neurological myelination metaphor — each high-quality repetition improves synaptic efficiency along the specific neural pathway used for that movement. Repetitions performed while fatigued use compensated motor patterns and potentially "grease" the wrong groove.

## Core Principle

**Sub-maximal practice at high frequency, never to failure.**

If your maximum rep count is N:
- Each GTG set: 40–60% of N (round down)
- Sessions per day: 2–10 (passive greasing)
- Days per week: 5–7
- Rest between sets: none required — spread throughout the day naturally

## Example Implementation

| Current Pull-up Max | GTG Set Size | Practice Frequency |
|--------------------|--------------|-------------------|
| 5 | 2–3 reps | 5–8 times/day |
| 10 | 4–6 reps | 4–6 times/day |
| 15 | 6–9 reps | 3–5 times/day |
| 20+ | 8–12 reps | 2–4 times/day |

## Who This Is For

- Beginners trying to achieve their first pull-up (start at negatives or band-assisted at 40–60% difficulty)
- Intermediate practitioners trying to double their pull-up max (e.g., from 8 to 15+)
- Athletes who train in a context with built-in natural rest (home workers, office setups with bars installed)
- Anyone on a deload from a structured program who wants to maintain movement frequency without load

## Practical Setup

The method works best when the movement is practiced **opportunistically** rather than as a scheduled workout:

- Install a pull-up bar in a frequently traversed doorway
- Set a push-up station near a work area
- Perform a set each time you pass the station or during natural breaks
- Do not "count" total daily reps — simply practice whenever the opportunity arises

## Interaction With Other Training

GTG is designed to run **alongside** other training, not replace it. Common combinations:

- GTG pull-ups + structured leg/core training (GTG does not interfere with leg recovery)
- GTG push-ups + structured pulling program (the sub-maximal load does not create meaningful recovery demand)

Do **not** attempt GTG on a movement you are currently training to failure in a structured program — the sub-maximal principle requires that the daily sets never approach the fatigue threshold. If GTG sets begin to feel difficult, either reduce rep count or rest a day.

## Performance Testing

**Test maximum performance infrequently** — once per 4–6 weeks. Daily testing destroys the sub-maximal principle and accumulates fatigue, negating the method's mechanism. The improvement in max count tends to be surprising even without frequent testing.

## Key Rules

- **Never train to failure or near failure on any GTG set.** A set where you struggle on the last rep is too heavy. Reduce reps.
- **Technical standard must match competition standard.** Dead-hang pull-ups with full range of motion — not chin-ups, not partial range. The neural pathway being grooved must match the target skill.
- **Spread practice throughout the day, not in a single session.** Clustering all daily GTG sets into one period converts it into a standard training session and reintroduces fatigue-state practice.

> GTG is most effective as a 4–8 week focused block targeting a single movement. After achieving the target, transition to a structured program like `bwf_recommended_routine` or `overcoming_gravity_template` to build balanced strength. Skill specificity: `connective_tissue`, `load_management`.

# Gymnastic Bodies Foundation One

Gymnastic Bodies Foundation One was developed by Christopher Sommer, a junior Olympic gymnastics coach with over 40 years of competitive coaching experience. The program adapts Olympic-level gymnastics preparation protocols for adult athletes, with specific modifications to account for the reduced connective tissue plasticity and existing movement limitations that distinguish adults from juvenile gymnasts.

The system's most significant structural departure from standard calisthenics programming is the **elimination of passive rest**. Instead of resting between strength sets, Foundation One pairs every strength exercise with a specific targeted mobility drill. The strength set conditions the muscle; the mobility set develops the active range of motion needed to express that strength. This creates a training model where every session simultaneously advances both strength and flexibility within the same time budget.

## Design Philosophy

Foundation One is based on Sommer's observation that adult athletes fail to progress in gymnastics skills not because of insufficient strength, but because of **insufficient active range of motion at the positions where the strength is required**: a planche requires overhead shoulder mobility and anterior serratus strength simultaneously; a front lever requires posterior chain flexibility and scapular depression strength together. These cannot be developed separately and then combined — they must be trained together in the positions where they will be used.

## Who This Is For

- Beginners to intermediate athletes motivated by gymnastics-style body control
- Athletes who want to develop simultaneous strength and mobility (not separate training blocks)
- Athletes with access to gymnastics rings and a pull-up bar
- Athletes willing to commit to a longer timeline (12–18+ months to full Foundation One completion)

## 4-Day Weekly Schedule

| Day | Strength Focus | Paired Mobility |
|-----|---------------|-----------------|
| Day 1 | Pull-ups + Ring Support + Hollow Body | Thoracic extension + Shoulder flexion |
| Day 2 | Squat progressions + Plank tension | Hip flexor + Ankle dorsiflexion |
| Day 3 | HSPU progressions + Scapular pulls | Wrist extension + Lat flexibility |
| Day 4 | Ring dips + Pulling skill | Shoulder external rotation + Thoracic rotation |

## The Active Recovery Pair Format

Each work set is performed, followed immediately by the prescribed mobility drill as the rest interval. Example for pull-ups:

1. Pull-up set (to prescribed reps or hold) → rest prescribed seconds → mobility drill
2. [Thoracic extension mobility: 60 s over foam roller]
3. Repeat for all sets

The mobility drill addresses the specific range-of-motion limitation that restricts the strength exercise. The total set-rest period is filled by the mobility drill — no passive sitting.

## Nine-Step Progression Structure

Each primary movement uses a 9-step ladder with advancement only when:
- The current step's standard is met for all prescribed sets
- Form is fully controlled (no compensation, no rushing)
- Standard is met consistently across multiple sessions (not a single peak performance)

Steps 1–3 are foundational (prerequisites), Steps 4–6 are intermediate, Steps 7–9 are advanced. Most athletes take 2–4 months per step at the foundational level.

## Key Rules

- **Wrist conditioning precedes all weight-bearing on the hands.** Foundation One includes a dedicated wrist prep series that must be established before ring dips, HSPU, or any significant wrist extension load. Skipping this is the primary cause of wrist injuries in the program.
- **Passive rest is replaced by mobility, not reduced.** The total rest period between strength sets is equivalent to a standard passive rest — it is used productively. Rushing through the mobility drill to start the next strength set defeats the system.
- **The 9-step progression is not linear for all athletes.** Early steps may take weeks; later steps often consolidate faster because the structural foundation is established. Do not project a timeline from early step progress.

> Preceding program: `bwf_recommended_routine`. For skill-specific advanced progressions: `calisthenics_advanced_skills`. Connective tissue management: see Shaw 2017 protocol in `connective_tissue`.

# Overcoming Gravity Skill Progression Template

*Overcoming Gravity* (2nd edition, 2016) by Steven Low is the most systematic publicly available text on calisthenics periodization and gymnastics-derived skill training. It differs from milestone-only programs like the BWF Recommended Routine by embedding skill progressions within a **periodized framework** that explicitly manages push/pull volume balance, connective tissue adaptation timelines, and skill density.

The system's central insight is that **advanced calisthenics skills fail not because of insufficient strength but because of imbalanced programming**: excessive pulling without horizontal push counterbalance causes elbow tendinopathy; excessive skill volume without structured deloads causes connective tissue overuse; and missing hollowbody mechanics limit every planche and front lever progression regardless of raw strength.

This template represents the program structure Low presents for intermediate athletes targeting the muscle-up, handstand push-up, and front lever/planche foundation skills.

## Who This Is For

- Athletes who have completed BWF Recommended Routine or equivalent
- Minimum baselines: 10 strict pull-ups, 5 parallel bar dips, 15-second dead hang, hollow body hold 30 s
- Skill targets: ring muscle-up, wall HSPU, tuck front lever, tuck planche
- Training age 1–2+ years

## 3-Day Weekly Schedule

| Day | Vertical Pull Pair | Horizontal Pull Pair | Push Skill | Core |
|-----|-------------------|---------------------|-----------|------|
| Day 1 | Vertical pull + HSPU progression | Inverted row + horizontal push | Ring dips | Hollow body |
| Day 2 | Weighted pull-up | Inverted row | Parallel dips + pushup variations | Pallof press |
| Day 3 | Vertical pull + HSPU progression | Inverted row | Ring dips | Hollow body |

Rest at least one day between sessions. Friday/Saturday are the most common rest days for a Mon/Wed/Fri structure.

## Session Structure

OG uses a **superset-style pairing** system where antagonist movements are paired with 60–90s rest between exercises:

1. **Warm-up** (5 min): scapular pull-ups + wrist prep + hollow body holds
2. **Pair A** — Vertical Pull + Vertical Push: 3–5 rounds
3. **Pair B** — Horizontal Pull + Horizontal Push: 3–5 rounds
4. **Skill practice** — 10–15 min on current skill target (muscle-up / HSPU / planche / front lever)
5. **Core finisher**: hollow body + anti-rotation

## Skill Day Structure (Pair Format)

Each skill pair performs A, rest 60–90s, B, rest 60–90s, then repeat:

| Pair | Exercise A | Exercise B |
|------|-----------|-----------|
| Pair A | Vertical Pull tier | Ring Dips or HSPU |
| Pair B | Inverted Row tier | Push-up progression tier |

## Push/Pull Volume Balance (Non-Negotiable)

The most common OG programming mistake is overloading pulling work. Approximate targets:

| Push type | Volume | Pull type | Volume |
|-----------|--------|-----------|--------|
| Vertical push (dips + HSPU) | 15–25 reps/session | Vertical pull | 15–25 reps/session |
| Horizontal push | 15–25 reps/session | Horizontal pull | 15–25 reps/session |

If vertical pulling significantly outpaces pushing, reduce pull volume or add push volume before the next session.

## Connective Tissue Management

OG is explicit that the connective tissue timeline for planche and front lever is **6–18 months** of consistent work — far exceeding the 4–8 weeks required for muscle strength gains at equivalent positions. Management protocols:

- Progress skill difficulty only when current tier is stable for ≥ 3 consecutive sessions
- Deload every 8–12 weeks: 40% volume reduction, one tier easier
- Stop every set 2 reps before technical failure — failed attempts in skill positions reinforce incorrect motor patterns
- If elbow, wrist, or shoulder discomfort develops: reduce skill difficulty by two tiers immediately

## Progression Ladder Quick Reference

| Skill | Entry | Intermediate | Goal |
|-------|-------|-------------|------|
| Front Lever | Tuck (10 s) | Advanced Tuck → One-leg | Straddle (8 s) |
| Planche | Tuck Push-up | Pseudo Planche Push-up | Straddle Hold (5 s) |
| HSPU | Pike Push-up | Wall HSPU (partial) | Wall HSPU (full ROM) |
| Muscle-up | Bar Negatives | Band-assisted | Strict Ring Muscle-up |

## Key Rules

- **Balance is not optional.** Push and pull volume must remain in approximate balance within each session. Imbalance accumulates across weeks and manifests as elbow or shoulder pain.
- **Skill practice belongs at the beginning of the session.** Low is explicit: skill work requires a fresh CNS. Attempting skill progressions after strength sets produces lower-quality practice and reinforces compensated patterns.
- **Never skip scapular activation.** The scapular pull-up warm-up is a health maintenance protocol, not warm-up filler.

> Preceding program: `bwf_recommended_routine`. Advanced skill progression: `calisthenics_advanced_skills`. Connective tissue management: `connective_tissue`, `load_management`.

# CompTrain Class

Developed by Ben Bergeron — coach of four CrossFit Games Champions — CompTrain Class is designed to deliver elite-level training adaptations within a standard 60-minute community class format. Unlike two-a-day competitor programs, CompTrain Class integrates strength, skill, and conditioning into single sessions that a working adult can complete without dedicating the entire day to training. The program has been the foundation for athletes who have qualified for and performed at the CrossFit Games while training in a class environment.

## Who This Is For

- Intermediate CrossFit athletes training in a class environment (not two-a-days)
- Athletes who have completed CrossFit Foundations and have basic gymnastics (kipping pull-up, double-unders, toes-to-bar, box jumps)
- Working adults who can commit 5 days per week at 60 minutes per session
- Not for: athletes who want to add supplemental volume outside of class (that's what HWPO or Invictus is for)

## Weekly Schedule

| Day | Strength / Skill | Metcon | Time Domain |
|-----|-----------------|--------|-------------|
| Monday | Back squat (wave loading) + Power clean technique | Couplet or triplet | 8–15 min |
| Tuesday | Overhead press or gymnastics skill | Sprint WOD | 3–8 min |
| Wednesday | Deadlift variation | Long chipper or AMRAP | 15–22 min |
| Thursday | Back squat (volume day) + gymnastic skill | Threshold couplet | 10–16 min |
| Friday | Power clean cycling | Sprint + chipper combo | 8–15 min |
| — | Saturday / Sunday | Rest or active recovery | — |

## Strength Wave (4-Week Cycle)

| Week | Squat Loading | Character |
|------|--------------|-----------|
| 1 | 60–65% × 5×5 | Deload; establish technique baseline |
| 2 | 70–75% × 5×5 | Build; add velocity intent to each rep |
| 3 | 80–85% × 5×3 | Strong; 2 RIR on working sets |
| 4 | 88–93% × 3×2 + PR attempt | Peak week; test new 1RM or 3RM |

## Sample Benchmark WODs

**Sprint Format (Tuesday):**
"Fran" — 21-15-9 Thrusters (43/29 kg) + Pull-ups. Target: ≤7 min intermediate, ≤4 min advanced.

**Threshold Format (Thursday):**
3 rounds: 500m Row + 12 Deadlifts (102/70 kg) + 21 Box Jumps (24/20 in). Target: 14–17 min.

**Long Chipper (Wednesday):**
For time: 100 Double-Unders → 50 Wall Balls → 40 TTB → 30 Power Cleans (60/43 kg) → 20 Pull-ups → 10 Handstand Push-ups. Target: 16–22 min.

## Scaling Philosophy

CompTrain provides three tiers of scaling within each workout:
- **Rx**: Standard weights and movements as prescribed
- **Scaled**: Weight reduction and movement substitution (e.g., ring rows for pull-ups)
- **Modified**: Further scaling for athletes recovering from injury or new to gymnastics

All scaling decisions prioritize the *intended stimulus* — a sprint WOD scaled so the athlete finishes in the correct time domain, not a grind at a heavier load.

## Progression

- **Strength**: 4-week wave cycles; re-test 1RM at the end of Week 4, then begin new cycle
- **Benchmark WODs**: Log every metcon score; compare on re-test after 4–6 weeks
- **Skill milestones**: Progress gymnastics skills one step each cycle (ring row → kipping pull-up → C2B; pike → box HSPU → kipping HSPU)

> Related programs: `crossfit_intermediate` (prerequisite), `hwpo_training` (advanced progression). Related guides: `crossfit_intermediate_guide`.

# CrossFit Aerobic Base Builder

Most CrossFit athletes plateau because they undertrain their aerobic system while overemphasizing high-intensity glycolytic work. The paradox is that the aerobic engine is the recovery mechanism for all other energy systems — a stronger oxidative base means faster phosphocreatine resynthesis between WOD rounds, faster lactate clearance between metcon efforts, and sustained power output across long workouts. This 8-week program temporarily deprioritizes strength PR chasing and gymnastics skill progression in favor of systematically building the aerobic engine.

## Who This Is For

- CrossFit intermediate athletes who breathe hard through all WODs regardless of length (aerobic engine is the limiter, not strength)
- Athletes who consistently underperform on workouts >10 minutes relative to their performance on short sprint WODs
- Athletes who have completed a 6–12 month base CrossFit program and identified aerobic capacity as their primary weakness
- Not for: athletes whose limiter is clearly strength (back squat <1.5× BW) or gymnastics (no kipping pull-up) — fix those first

## The Zone 2 Rationale

Zone 2 is defined as the maximum intensity at which an athlete can sustain nasal breathing, or approximately 60–70% of maximum heart rate (RPE 2–3 of 10). At this intensity:
- Mitochondrial biogenesis is maximized (PGC-1α signaling)
- Type I slow-twitch fibers are selectively trained for fat oxidation
- Lactate is produced and cleared at roughly equal rates (lactate ≈ 1.5–2.0 mmol/L)
- No meaningful CNS or glycogen demand accumulates

Consistent Zone 2 training for 6–8 weeks produces measurable increases in lactate threshold and VO₂max — the physiological substrate that determines how hard an athlete can work for 10+ minutes.

## Weekly Schedule

| Day | Session Type | Duration/Volume |
|-----|-------------|----------------|
| Monday | Zone 2 Row | 25–35 min |
| Tuesday | Strength maintenance (squat + deadlift) + Zone 2 Bike | 30 min bike |
| Wednesday | Threshold intervals (row or bike) | 4–6 × 4–6 min at threshold |
| Thursday | Zone 2 Row or Bike | 30–40 min |
| Friday | Combined WOD (aerobic pace, not sprint) | 20–25 min at Zone 2–3 |
| Saturday/Sunday | Rest or optional easy run (Zone 1, truly easy) | ≤30 min |

## 8-Week Volume Progression

| Week | Total Aerobic Volume | Threshold Work | Character |
|------|---------------------|---------------|-----------|
| 1 | 60 min | None (baseline) | Establish pace |
| 2 | 80 min | 2 × 10 min at threshold | Build base |
| 3 | 100 min | 2 × 12 min | Load week |
| 4 | 90 min | 2 × 8 min | Recovery week |
| 5 | 120 min | 3 × 10 min | Build 2 |
| 6 | 140 min | 3 × 12 min | Peak volume |
| 7 | 120 min | 2 × 10 min | Reduce |
| 8 | 80 min | None (taper) | Test week |

## Threshold Intervals

Threshold pace is 15–20% harder than Zone 2, approximately RPE 6–7 of 10:
- **Row**: ~2:03–2:10/500m for male intermediate
- **Bike**: 250–280W for male intermediate
- Structure: work interval → equal rest → repeat; e.g., 4×6 min / 6 min rest

## End-of-Program Benchmark Tests

Test the following before (Week 1) and after (Week 8) to measure aerobic adaptation:

| Test | Improvement Indicator |
|------|----------------------|
| 2K Row | Time improvement ≥15 sec |
| 5K Run | Time improvement ≥45 sec |
| Cindy (20 min AMRAP) | +2–3 rounds |
| 5-round metcon (e.g., 5×400m row + 10 pull-ups) | Time improvement ≥60 sec |

> Prerequisites: `crossfit_foundations`, `crossfit_intermediate`. Related guides: `crossfit_intermediate_guide`.

# CrossFit Competition Preparation

Advanced CrossFit programming assumes the athlete has mastered fundamental movement patterns and is targeting sanctioned competition (CrossFit Open, Sanctionals, or regional events). Training is organized into 16-week blocks with two-a-day sessions on most days — AM for strength and skill work, PM for conditioning. The program develops all four CrossFit fitness domains simultaneously: maximal strength, metabolic conditioning, gymnastic capacity, and Olympic lifting efficiency.

## Who This Is For

- Athletes competing in CrossFit Open or higher-level sanctioned events
- Minimum baselines: Snatch ≥ 0.9× BW, Clean & Jerk ≥ 1.1× BW, Back Squat ≥ 1.5× BW
- Capable of: 20+ unbroken pull-ups, 10+ ring dips, consistent double-unders
- Can handle and recover from two daily training sessions

## 16-Week Block Structure

| Block | Weeks | Strength Focus | Conditioning |
|-------|-------|---------------|-------------|
| Accumulation | 1–5 | 3–5 reps @ 75–85% | Long, aerobic-dominant WODs |
| Intensification | 6–10 | 1–3 reps @ 85–95% | Mixed intensity, benchmark testing |
| Peaking | 11–14 | Max testing | Competition simulation |
| Taper | 15–16 | 50% volume, maintain intensity | Short, sharp competition-pace WODs |

## Daily Structure (Two-a-Day)

**AM Session (90 min)**:
1. Warm-up + mobility (15 min)
2. Strength or Olympic lift work (30–40 min)
3. Skill gymnastics (20–25 min)
4. Short conditioning piece (10–15 min)

**PM Session (60–75 min)**:
1. Warm-up (10 min)
2. Primary conditioning WOD (20–45 min)
3. Accessory work (10–15 min)

Minimum 4–6 hours between AM and PM sessions.

## Weekly Template

| Day | AM | PM |
|-----|----|----|
| Mon | Back Squat + Snatch technique | Pull-up capacity + metcon |
| Tue | Deadlift + Overhead Press | Clean & Jerk cycling + metcon |
| Wed | Snatch (heavy singles) + Front Squat | Gymnastics skill + benchmark |
| Thu | Clean & Jerk (heavy) + Deadlift | Hero WOD or long chipper |
| Fri | Back Squat + Overhead Squat + Snatch | Short, heavy couplet |
| Sat | Clean & Jerk + Ring Dips | Aerobic Zone 2 (45–60 min) |
| Sun | Rest | |

## Competition Week Protocol

Weeks 15–16: 50% volume reduction. Keep all intensity cues (same loads, same gymnastics standards). Short, sharp workouts only. Practice competition movements at competition weight (not max effort). Full rest 2 days before competition.

> Entry point: `crossfit_intermediate`. For supporting aerobic base see `aerobic_base`, `concurrent_training`. For Olympic lift technique foundation see `olympic_weightlifting_beginner`.

# CrossFit Foundations

CrossFit is a strength and conditioning system built on constantly varied, functional movements performed at high intensity. The Foundations program introduces the ten physical domains (cardiovascular/respiratory endurance, stamina, strength, flexibility, power, speed, coordination, agility, balance, accuracy) through a structured 8-week on-ramp.

## Weekly Template

| Day | Component 1 | Component 2 | Component 3 |
|-----|-------------|-------------|-------------|
| Monday | Strength (squat) | Skill | WOD - Couplet |
| Tuesday | Gymnastics skill | Monostructural cardio | WOD - Triplet |
| Wednesday | Rest / active recovery | | |
| Thursday | Strength (press or pull) | Olympic lift intro | WOD - AMRAP |
| Friday | Strength (hinge) | Skill | WOD - For Time |
| Saturday | Long aerobic WOD (partner) | | |
| Sunday | Rest | | |

## Landmark WODs (Benchmark Workouts)

CrossFit tracks fitness through named benchmark workouts. Key ones introduced in Foundations:

| WOD | Format | Movements |
|-----|--------|-----------|
| Cindy | 20-min AMRAP | 5 pull-ups, 10 push-ups, 15 air squats |
| Fran | For time | 21-15-9 thrusters (43 kg) + pull-ups |
| Grace | For time | 30 clean-and-jerks (61 kg) |
| Helen | 3 rounds for time | 400 m run, 21 KB swings, 12 pull-ups |
| Murph | For time | 1-mile run, 100 pull-ups, 200 push-ups, 300 squats, 1-mile run |

## Rep Schemes for Strength Components

| Scheme | Application |
|--------|-------------|
| 5x5 | Linear strength (squat, press, deadlift) |
| 3-3-3-3-3 | Wave loading, heavier than 5x5 |
| EMOM 10 (2 reps) | Technique-focused Olympic lifts |
| 3x8-12 | Accessory / gymnastics volume |

## Scaling Principles

- **Rx**: Prescribed weight and movement standard
- **Scaled**: Reduced load, reduced range, or movement regression
- **Beginner**: High regression - banded, bodyweight, or reduced volume

The rule: choose a scale that allows the intended metabolic stimulus (e.g., Fran should take 3-8 min, not 20 min).

## Ten Physical Domains Addressed

Cardiovascular endurance, stamina, strength, flexibility, power, speed, coordination, agility, balance, accuracy.

WOD programming intentionally rotates emphasis across all ten. No single domain is isolated; most WODs develop 3-5 simultaneously.

> CrossFit GPP base for sport-specific carry-over. For pure strength, see `texas_method` or `5_3_1`. For Olympic lifting emphasis within CrossFit, see `catalyst_12_week`.

# CrossFit Intermediate Strength & Conditioning

A structured 5-day program for athletes who have completed CrossFit Foundations and developed basic movement patterns. The intermediate stage shifts from learning movements to developing *capacity* within them: increasing strength on barbell lifts, building higher-rep gymnastics capacity, and improving metabolic conditioning benchmarks over time.

## Who This Is For

- Athletes who have completed CrossFit Foundations or 3–6 months of regular CrossFit
- Can perform: kipping pull-ups, basic barbell cycling (power clean, overhead press), double-unders
- Wants structured progression rather than randomized daily programming

## Weekly Schedule

| Day | Strength | Conditioning |
|-----|----------|-------------|
| Mon | Back Squat 5×3 + Ring Dips 3×max | AMRAP 12: Pull-ups / Pushups / Air Squats |
| Tue | Overhead Press 5×3 + Power Clean 5×3 | Couplet or triplet, 10–15 min |
| Wed | Conventional Deadlift 5×3 + Romanian DL 3×8 | Chipper or hero WOD (20–30 min) |
| Thu | Front Squat 4×4 + Back Squat 3×10 | EMOM 20: mixed monostructural |
| Fri | Push Press 5×3 + Power Clean cycling | Benchmark (5 rounds for time) |
| Sat–Sun | Rest or aerobic Zone 2 (see `aerobic_base`) | |

## Strength Work Protocol

Strength blocks precede conditioning each day. Warm up to working weight, complete all prescribed strength sets at the given percentage, then transition directly to conditioning after a short rest. This order ensures strength quality is not compromised by prior fatigue.

## Conditioning Structure

Each week includes:
- **1 AMRAP** (as many rounds as possible): tracks total work capacity
- **1 Benchmark WOD** (Girls, Heroes, or named workout): trackable over time
- **1 Chipper or long WOD**: engine work, sustained output
- **1 EMOM** (every minute on the minute): power output consistency
- **1 Couplet/Triplet**: high-intensity short effort

Keep conditioning scores. Re-test the same benchmark every 4 weeks to track improvement.

## Gymnastics Development

Gymnastics capacity grows from strict to kipping to butterfly on pull-ups, and from basic ring support to ring dips to muscle-up progressions. Prioritize:
1. Strict capacity before adding kipping volume
2. Ring dips before ring muscle-up attempts
3. Hollow body position as the foundation for all gymnastics

## Benchmark Targets at Program Completion

| Movement | Entry standard | 12-week goal |
|----------|---------------|-------------|
| Back Squat | 1.0× BW | 1.15× BW |
| Strict Press | 0.55× BW | 0.65× BW |
| Power Clean | 0.75× BW | 0.85× BW |
| Pull-ups (unbroken) | 10 | 15+ |
| Ring Dips | 5 | 10+ |

> For beginner CrossFit: `crossfit_foundations`. For concurrent training considerations: `concurrent_training`. For aerobic base development: `aerobic_base`.

# CrossFit Open Prep Cycle

This 8-week peaking cycle is designed to be inserted immediately before the CrossFit Open begins. It does not introduce new training stimuli — the goal is to *express* existing fitness, eliminate accumulated fatigue, and sharpen the specific skills and WOD formats that the Open demands. Athletes who attempt to get fitter during Open prep tend to arrive at competition week overtrained rather than peaked.

## Who This Is For

- Intermediate to advanced CrossFit athletes (6+ months of structured training) who want to peak specifically for the Open
- Athletes who have been training consistently through an off-season or base-building program for 3–6 months prior
- Not for: athletes just starting CrossFit or who do not have basic gymnastics skills established

## 8-Week Structure

| Phase | Weeks | Volume | Intensity | Focus |
|-------|-------|--------|-----------|-------|
| Intensification | 1–3 | High | 90–100% | Open-format WODs, skill sharpening |
| Peak | 4–5 | Moderate-high | 100% | Competition simulation, benchmark PRs |
| Taper | 6–7 | Low (−40%) | Maintained | Fatigue clearance, no new stimuli |
| Competition | 8 | Minimal | High (2 sessions) | Rest and activate before Open begins |

## Open WOD Movement Priority List

Based on analysis of Open workouts from 2011–2024, the following movements appear most frequently and should receive specific prep:

| Movement | Open Frequency | Prep Priority |
|----------|---------------|---------------|
| Pull-ups / C2B pull-ups | Very High | Critical |
| Double-unders | Very High | Critical |
| Wall balls | Very High | Critical |
| Toes-to-bar | High | Critical |
| Thrusters | High | Critical |
| Rowing | Moderate-High | Important |
| Power cleans / barbell cycling | Moderate-High | Important |
| Handstand push-ups | Moderate | Important |
| Box jumps / box jump-overs | Moderate | Standard |
| Muscle-ups (bar or ring) | Low-Moderate | Advanced only |

## Weekly Schedule (Weeks 1–3: Intensification)

| Day | Session Content |
|-----|----------------|
| Monday | Back squat maintenance (3×3 at 90%) + Open sprint WOD (3–7 min) |
| Tuesday | Gymnastics skill block (TTB + pull-ups + HSPU) + threshold WOD (10–16 min) |
| Wednesday | Barbell cycling (power clean + thruster) + Open AMRAP simulation (20 min) |
| Thursday | Strength maintenance (press + deadlift, 2×3 each at 88%) + sprint WOD |
| Friday | Full Open simulation: announce, prep, execute as competition, score |
| Saturday/Sunday | Rest or light aerobic (30 min Zone 2) |

## Competition Week (Week 8)

- **Monday**: Light row (15 min easy aerobic) + 3×10 each: pull-ups, TTB, DUs — activation only
- **Tuesday**: Power clean singles at 80% 1RM (5 singles) + 2 short Open-paced sets — prime the system
- **Wednesday–Thursday**: Full rest
- **Friday**: Open Workout 1

## Taper Rules

- Volume reduction: −40% working sets Weeks 6–7 (maintain frequency)
- Intensity: unchanged — every set at same load/effort as Weeks 4–5
- No new movements or new stimuli in Weeks 6–8
- Nutrition: increase carbohydrates by 10–15% in Week 7 for glycogen supercompensation

> Prerequisite programs: `crossfit_intermediate` or `comptrain_class`. Related guides: `crossfit_intermediate_guide`, `crossfit_advanced_guide`.

# HWPO (Hard Work Pays Off)

Created by Mat Fraser — five-time CrossFit Games Champion — HWPO is structured around a two-a-day framework that separates aerobic / gymnastics work (AM) from strength and high-intensity conditioning (PM). The guiding principle is that the AMPK pathway activated by aerobic conditioning in the AM must return to baseline before the PM session stimulates mTOR-dependent strength and hypertrophy adaptations; a minimum 4–6 hour gap (8 hours optimal) between sessions is non-negotiable.

## Who This Is For

- Advanced CrossFit athletes aiming at Open top 10% placement or sanctional qualification
- Athletes who have completed an intermediate program and have established gymnastics fundamentals (kipping pull-ups, toes-to-bar, kipping HSPU, double-unders)
- Prerequisites: Back squat ≥ 2.0× BW, Power clean ≥ 1.0× BW, Fran ≤ 5:00

## Weekly Schedule

| Session | Day | AM Focus | PM Focus |
|---------|-----|----------|----------|
| Day 1 | Monday | Aerobic row + gymnastics (pull-ups, HSPU) | Back squat + Olympic + Metcon |
| Day 2 | Tuesday | Zone 2 run/row + snatch technique | Clean & jerk + Deadlift + Metcon |
| Day 3 | Wednesday | Aerobic row + gymnastics (rings, TTB) | Back squat + Olympic + Metcon |
| Day 4 | Thursday | Zone 2 bike/row + C&J technique | Overhead press + Deadlift + Metcon |
| Day 5 | Friday | Aerobic row + gymnastics (HSPU, pull-ups) | Back squat + Olympic + Metcon |
| — | Saturday | Active recovery or long aerobic (60–90 min easy row) | — |
| — | Sunday | Full rest | — |

## Block Periodization

**Block 1 (Weeks 1–4) — Aerobic Base & Strength Accumulation**

| Component | Volume/Intensity |
|-----------|-----------------|
| AM aerobic | 20–40 min Zone 2 daily |
| Strength (Squat, DL) | 5×5 at 70–80% 1RM |
| Olympic lifting | Technique focus 70–80% |
| Metcon time domain | 12–20 min aerobic threshold |

**Block 2 (Weeks 5–8) — Threshold & Strength Intensification**

| Component | Volume/Intensity |
|-----------|-----------------|
| AM aerobic | 15–25 min + 1–2 interval sessions/week |
| Strength | 5×3 at 80–90% 1RM |
| Olympic lifting | Heavy singles 85–90% |
| Metcon time domain | 6–12 min lactic threshold |

**Block 3 (Weeks 9–12) — Open Peaking & Realization**

| Component | Volume/Intensity |
|-----------|-----------------|
| AM aerobic | 10–20 min; reduce volume 40% |
| Strength | 3×1–2 at 90–97% 1RM |
| Olympic lifting | Competition singles |
| Metcon time domain | 2–8 min peak intensity |

## Sample WOD Formats (PM Metcons)

**Sprint (Lactic, 3–6 min):**
"Grace" — 30 Clean & Jerks for time @ 60/43 kg

**Threshold (8–15 min):**
5 rounds: 10 Toes-to-Bar + 10 Wall Balls + 10 Box Jumps

**Aerobic Chipper (18–25 min):**
For time: 1K Row → 50 Double-Unders → 30 Pull-ups → 20 Power Cleans (70/47 kg) → 10 Bar Muscle-ups

## Progression

- **Strength**: Wave loading across blocks (accumulation → intensification → realization)
- **Benchmark WODs**: Re-test same benchmark every 4 weeks; time or score improvement = progression
- **Two-a-day recovery signal**: If PM session quality drops on >2 consecutive days, reduce AM session duration by 30%; restore PM quality before resuming AM volume

> Prerequisite programs: `crossfit_intermediate`, `crossfit_competition_prep`. Related guides: `crossfit_advanced_guide`.

# Invictus Athlete

CJ Martin's Invictus Athlete program is built on a 16-week periodized block structure that explicitly separates training phases by dominant energy system — a more systematic approach to concurrent training than the daily-mixed format used by most CrossFit programs. The program is organized around the principle that aerobic adaptation and strength adaptation cannot be optimally pursued simultaneously within the same short training period; Invictus dedicates entire 4-week blocks to one quality at a time, while maintaining all others at minimum effective dose.

## Who This Is For

- Advanced CrossFit athletes competing in the Open and aiming for sanctional qualifying (top 5%)
- Athletes who have specific identifiable weaknesses to target (aerobic base, strength, gymnastics)
- Prerequisites: Back squat ≥ 2.0× BW, Clean & jerk ≥ 1.0× BW, Snatch ≥ 0.8× BW, Fran ≤ 4:30, ring muscle-up established
- Two-a-day training capacity: minimum 4–6 hours between sessions

## 16-Week Block Structure

**Block 1 (Weeks 1–4): Aerobic Foundation**
- AM: Long Zone 2 aerobic sessions (30–45 min row/run/bike) + strict gymnastics
- PM: Strength accumulation (5×5) + aerobic threshold metcons (15–22 min)
- Goal: Raise aerobic ceiling — every other quality is supported by oxidative base

**Block 2 (Weeks 5–8): Strength Accumulation**
- AM: Olympic lifting technique + interval conditioning (4×5 min at threshold)
- PM: Heavy strength (5×4 at 75–85%) + moderate metcons (10–18 min)
- Goal: Build strength reserve to reduce metcon economy cost

**Block 3 (Weeks 9–12): Sport-Specific Intensification**
- AM: Olympic lifting heavy singles + lactic sprint capacity (30–90 sec max efforts)
- PM: Near-max strength (5×3 at 82–90%) + Open-format WODs (2–12 min)
- Goal: Peak lactic power and sport-specific skill under fatigue

**Block 4 (Weeks 13–16): Competition Peak**
- AM: Reduced volume; skill maintenance only
- PM: Taper; 90–95% intensity on all main lifts; competition-format WODs only
- Goal: Express fitness; arrive at competition at peak with cleared fatigue

## Weekly Schedule (Block 2 Example)

| Session | AM | PM |
|---------|----|----|
| Monday | Clean & jerk technique (5 singles at 82%) | Back squat 5×4 at 78% + Long metcon |
| Tuesday | Snatch technique + row intervals | Deadlift 4×3 at 83% + Short metcon |
| Wednesday | Gymnastics skill (muscle-ups, HSPU) | Back squat 5×4 at 80% + Threshold WOD |
| Thursday | Snatch cycling + aerobic run | C&J heavy + Overhead press + Metcon |
| Friday | Gymnastics + pull-up volume | Back squat 5×4 at 82% + Sprint WOD |
| Saturday | Long row (40 min aerobic) or benchmark test | Rest |
| Sunday | Rest | Rest |

## Benchmark Testing (Start and End of Each Block)

| Benchmark | Purpose |
|-----------|---------|
| 2K Row | Aerobic power indicator |
| Fran | Lactic/strength integration |
| Max unbroken butterfly pull-ups | Gymnastics capacity |
| Heavy power clean (1RM) | Barbell cycling ceiling |
| 1RM back squat | Strength reserve |

## Progression

- **Strength**: Undulating load within each block; increases 2–3% per week within block
- **Gymnastics**: Progress one tier per block (strict → kipping → weighted or deficit)
- **Energy systems**: Shift dominant system priority by block; maintain others at MEV

> Prerequisite programs: `crossfit_intermediate`, `hwpo_training`. Related guides: `crossfit_advanced_guide`.

# Bulgarian Method

Developed by Ivan Abadjiev as head coach of the Bulgarian national weightlifting team beginning in the 1970s, the Bulgarian Method is the most radical application of the specificity principle in strength athletics. Abadjiev eliminated all non-competition movements, arguing that any exercise not identical to the snatch, clean & jerk, or front squat introduces non-specific fatigue that competes with adaptation in those movements. The back squat was famously removed because the slightly altered back angle does not perfectly match the receiving position of the clean.

The system operates entirely through **daily maximum autoregulation**: no percentages, no fixed sets-and-reps schemes. Each session the athlete determines the day's ceiling through feel, works up to it, then continues with back-off singles. The high-frequency exposure — up to 12 sessions per week at full development — produces rapid CNS adaptation and motor pattern refinement.

## Design Philosophy — Why Daily Maxes and Nothing Else

The Bulgarian Method is built on two principles taken to their logical extremes:

**1. Absolute specificity.** Abadjiev's central argument: the body adapts to exactly what you do. A back squat is not a clean recovery — it trains a slightly different motor pattern, distributes load slightly differently across the posterior chain, and generates fatigue that competes with clean recovery adaptation. By eliminating every non-competition movement, every unit of training stress is directed toward competition performance. This is the specificity principle (see `specificity`) applied without compromise.

**2. Frequency over volume.** Instead of doing 5 sets of 3 at 85% in one session (traditional model), the Bulgarian athlete does 1 heavy single across 12 sessions per week. The total weekly volume may be similar, but each session's fatigue is lower, recovery between sessions is shorter, and the athlete gets 12 opportunities per week to practice the competition lift at near-maximal load. This accelerates motor learning and CNS adaptation.

**Why autoregulation instead of percentages?** At 12 sessions per week, day-to-day performance variation is enormous. A percentage-based program cannot account for the cumulative fatigue of daily maximal training. The daily max system solves this: the athlete's body tells the program what it can handle today. There is no "missed" workout — a daily max of 90% is as valid as one at 98%. The system self-corrects.

**Why it works for elite athletes but not most people:** The Bulgarian Method requires years of technique development before entry. If the athlete's snatch technique breaks down at 90%, daily maxes at 90%+ will reinforce bad patterns. The system only works when the technical foundation is so deep that maximal loads do not alter movement quality — a condition that typically requires 4+ years of dedicated Olympic lifting.

## Who This Is For

- Advanced competitive weightlifters with 4+ years of continuous structured training
- Athletes with established, reliable technique in both competition lifts under ≥ 90% load
- Athletes with confirmed joint health (patellar tendon, shoulder girdle)
- **Not appropriate for:** intermediate athletes, athletes with any chronic joint pain, athletes without daily access to a platform and experienced coaching

## Frequency Ramp-Up

| Development Phase | Sessions/Day | Sessions/Week |
|------------------|-------------|---------------|
| Entry (months 1–3) | 1 | 6 |
| Intermediate (months 4–8) | 1–2 (MWF double) | 9 |
| Full Bulgarian (months 9+) | 2 (Mon–Sat) | 12 |

Do not advance to the next frequency tier until daily maxes are stable across 3+ consecutive weeks at the current tier.

## Single-Session Day Structure

| Order | Movement | Protocol |
|-------|----------|----------|
| 1 | Snatch | Build to daily max; reduce 10–15%, continue singles |
| 2 | Clean & Jerk | Build to daily max; reduce 10–15%, continue singles |
| 3 | Front Squat | Build to daily max |

## Double-Session Day Structure (AM / PM)

| Session | Movement | Protocol |
|---------|----------|----------|
| AM | Snatch | Daily max + back-off singles |
| AM | Front Squat | Daily max |
| PM | Clean & Jerk | Daily max + back-off singles |
| PM | Front Squat | Daily max |

## The Daily Max Defined

The daily max is the heaviest weight achievable that day where:
- Technique is indistinguishable from competition-standard execution
- No grinding, visible strain, or positional breakdown
- No stimulants or deliberate arousal manipulation

A daily max that is lower than yesterday is valid data. Chronic suppression of daily maxes over multiple consecutive sessions is the recovery signal; rest or reduce frequency.

## Key Rules

- **Zero accessory work.** No rows, no Romanian deadlifts, no overhead press. No back squat. The Bulgarian system treats any non-specific movement as adaptation energy wasted.
- **Technical integrity is absolute.** A lift requiring positional compromise does not count as the daily max. Lower the bar and try again.
- **No forced PRs.** Attempting weights above the genuine daily max through psychological arousal defeats the autoregulatory mechanism entirely.

> For evidence-based context on pull intensities and periodization alternatives, see `olympic_intermediate_guide`. For periodized Olympic training at this level, see `olympic_weightlifting_advanced`.

# California Strength Daily Training Program

Developed by Dave Spitz at California Strength in the 2000s–2010s, this program represents a modern systematic evolution of Olympic lifting design. Its key innovation is structuring the intensity of daily maximum training within a fatigue-management framework — unlike the pure Bulgarian Method, Cal Strength integrates extensive general physical preparation (GPP) complexes and a structured weekly pattern of heavy and technical days.

The program is organized around a **daily undulating periodization** model: heavy classic lift days (MWF) alternate with technical recovery days (Tu/Th), with Saturday dedicated to maximal squatting volume ("Squaturday"). This structure allows the nervous system to express near-maximal force on heavy days while accumulating technical volume and accessory work on lighter days.

A distinguishing feature is the **mandatory accessory complex** system:
- **Complex A** (upper body structural balance): push press + bent-over rows, supersetted
- **Complex B** (posterior chain integrity): Romanian deadlift + hyperextensions, supersetted

These complexes address the anterior-posterior muscle imbalances and rotator cuff vulnerabilities that develop in athletes who perform nothing but Olympic lifting at high frequency.

## Who This Is For

- Advanced weightlifters who have outgrown weekly periodization models
- Athletes who can train 6 days/week with access to proper recovery (sleep, nutrition, soft tissue)
- Athletes with established technical proficiency in both competition lifts under 85%+ loads
- Not appropriate for: intermediate athletes, athletes with shoulder impingement or patellar tendon issues

## Weekly Schedule

| Day | Session Type | Primary Focus |
|-----|-------------|---------------|
| Monday | Heavy | Snatch + C&J daily max; Complex A |
| Tuesday | Technical | Power variations; Complex A + B |
| Wednesday | Heavy | Snatch + C&J daily max; Complex A |
| Thursday | Technical | Block work + complexes; Complex B |
| Friday | Heavy | Snatch + C&J daily max; Complex A |
| Saturday | Squaturday | 5RM back squat + drop sets |

## Heavy Day Structure (Mon / Wed / Fri)

| Exercise | Protocol | Notes |
|----------|----------|-------|
| Snatch | Daily max → 2–3 back-off doubles @85–90% | "Red Zone" max |
| Clean & Jerk | Daily max → 2–3 back-off doubles @85–90% | |
| Front Squat | 3 × 3 | @85–90% front squat 1RM |
| Snatch Pull or Clean Pull | 4 × 3 | 100–120% (phase-dependent) |
| Complex A | 4 rounds: Push Press + Bent-Over Row | Supersetted, moderate load |

## Technical Day Structure (Tue / Thu)

| Exercise | Protocol | Notes |
|----------|----------|-------|
| Power Snatch or Hang Snatch | 4 × 2–3 | @70–80%; positional work |
| Clean Complex (clean + FS + jerk from rack) | 4 × 1+1+1 | Pause in rack before jerk |
| Complex A | 3 rounds: Push Press + Bent-Over Row | Light; structural maintenance |
| Complex B | 3 rounds: Romanian Deadlift + Hyperextension | Posterior chain |

## Saturday — Squaturday

| Exercise | Protocol |
|----------|----------|
| Back Squat | Build to 5RM |
| Back Squat drop sets | 2 × 5 at 10% below 5RM |

## Key Rules

- **Back-off doubles are mandatory.** After every daily max, the back-off sets are not optional — they accumulate high-quality volume in a potentiated state while managing systemic fatigue.
- **Complex A and B are not optional accessories.** They exist to prevent the structural imbalances that cause injury in daily-max systems. Skipping them to save time erodes the structural foundation that protects the shoulder and posterior chain.
- **The daily max is bounded by technique, not by ambition.** A lift requiring any positional compromise is not the daily max.

> For lower-frequency periodized alternatives at this level, see `olympic_weightlifting_advanced`. For the autoregulated pure-specificity approach, see `bulgarian_method`.

# Catalyst Athletics 12-Week Traditional Cycle

Designed by Greg Everett (Catalyst Athletics) for intermediate Olympic weightlifters and functional fitness athletes. Trains 5 days per week: Mon / Tue / Wed (rest) / Thu / Fri / Sat, with Sunday as a passive rest day.

## Mesocycle Structure

| Phase | Weeks | Focus | Intensity | Volume |
|-------|-------|-------|-----------|--------|
| Accumulation | 1–4 | Work capacity, positional endurance | 60–75% | High (5×6, 4×3) |
| Transmutation | 5–8 | Neurological adaptation, heavier loads | 70–85% | Moderate |
| Realization | 9–12 | Peaking, competition performance | 85%+ | Low; ascending singles |

## Week 1 — Day-by-Day Prescription

| Day | Exercise | Sets × Reps | Intensity |
|-----|----------|-------------|-----------|
| Monday | Snatch | 1×3, 1×3, 4×3 | 60%, 65%, 70% |
| | Snatch Pull | 4×3 | 90% snatch 1RM |
| | Back Squat | 5×6 | 70% |
| | SLDL | 3×5 | by feel |
| Tuesday | Hang Muscle Snatch | 5×3 | by feel |
| | Power Snatch | 5×3 | 70% |
| | Push Press + OHS | 5×(5+1) | 70% snatch 1RM |
| Wednesday | Rest | — | — |
| Thursday | Clean & Jerk | 1×(3+1), 1×(3+1), 4×(3+1) | 60%, 65%, 70% |
| | Clean Pull | 4×3 | 90% clean 1RM |
| | Front Squat | 5×4 | 70% |
| Friday | Power Clean | 5×3 | 70% |
| | Power Jerk | 5×3 | 60% split jerk 1RM |
| | Push Press | 5×5 | 70% |
| Saturday | Snatch | 1×3, 2×3, 2×3, 4×1 | 65%, 70%, 75%, 80% |
| | Clean & Jerk | 1×(3+1), 2×(3+1), 3×(2+1), 4×(1+1) | 65%, 70%, 75%, 80% |
| | Back Squat | 5×3 | 75% |
| | Good Morning | 3×5 | by feel |
| | Press | 3×8 | by feel |
| Sunday | Rest | — | — |

## Week 2 Changes

Intensity increases ~5% on primary lifts. Pulling volume increases. Saturday extends to 85% singles on snatch and clean-and-jerk.

## Notation Guide

| Notation | Meaning |
|----------|---------|
| (3+1) | Three cleans from floor + one split jerk without re-racking |
| (5+1) | Five push presses + one overhead squat without re-racking |
| by feel | Load chosen based on daily readiness; technique takes priority over load |

## Autoregulation and Progression Rules

1. Good technique + bar speed on a set → add load for next set
2. Three consecutive misses at same weight → terminate all further attempts at that load
3. Personal record achieved → recalculate remaining session percentages from the new max
4. Test sessions at end of each mesocycle (weeks ~4, 8, 12) under near-competition conditions

> For technique notes on the competition lifts, see `exercises/snatch.md` and `exercises/clean_and_jerk.md`.

# Glenn Pendlay Weightlifting System

Glenn Pendlay was the most influential American Olympic weightlifting coach of his generation, responsible for developing several national champions and international-caliber athletes. His system represents a structured Western adaptation of Eastern European high-frequency methods, modified for athletes who lack the multi-decade developmental foundation of Soviet-bloc lifters.

Pendlay's central insight was that American athletes at the intermediate level cannot tolerate the pure high-intensity, low-variability volume of the Bulgarian method. They need a **strength base** — specifically a raw leg and upper-back strength reserve that stays far ahead of the technical demands of the competition lifts. A lifter who can squat 200 kg but only clean 120 kg has enormous room for technical improvement without requiring more pulling strength. A lifter whose squat matches their clean will stall the moment their technique improves.

The program uses a **weekly Texas Method structure**: Volume Day drives the adaptation stimulus, Recovery Day allows fatigue clearance while maintaining technical exposure, and Intensity Day expresses the accumulated strength as a heavy single.

## Who This Is For

- Intermediate lifters who have established sound technique in the squat snatch and squat clean
- Athletes who have completed a beginner program and are training 4 days/week consistently
- Athletes whose squat strength has begun to limit their competition lift progress
- Not appropriate for: beginners who have not yet established squat receiving positions

## Schedule (4 days/week)

| Day | Session | Primary Stress |
|-----|---------|---------------|
| Monday | Volume Day | High-volume snatch, squat, pulls, rows |
| Tuesday | C&J Volume | Clean & Jerk volume + supplementary |
| Wednesday | Recovery Day | Light snatch, front squat, push press |
| Friday | Intensity Day | Heavy snatch + C&J singles, heavy squats |

Thursday and the weekend are rest.

## Monday — Volume Day

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Intensity |
|----------|-------------|-----------|
| Snatch | 5 × 3 | 72–78% |
| Back Squat | 5 × 5 | 75–80% |
| Snatch Pull | 4 × 3 | 100–105% snatch 1RM |
| Clean Pull | 4 × 3 | 100–105% clean 1RM |
| Bent-Over Row | 4 × 5 | Moderate; dead-stop each rep |

## Tuesday — C&J Volume

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Intensity |
|----------|-------------|-----------|
| Clean & Jerk | 4 × 2 | 72–78% |
| Front Squat | 3 × 4 | 70–75% |
| Push Press | 4 × 5 | Moderate |

## Wednesday — Recovery Day

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Intensity |
|----------|-------------|-----------|
| Snatch | 4 × 2 | 65–70% |
| Snatch Pull | 3 × 3 | 90–95% |
| Front Squat | 4 × 3 | 70–75% |
| Push Press | 3 × 5 | Light |
| Bent-Over Row | 3 × 5 | Light |

## Friday — Intensity Day

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Intensity |
|----------|-------------|-----------|
| Snatch | Build to heavy single; 2–3 back-offs | Daily max → 85% back-offs |
| Clean & Jerk | Build to heavy single; 2 back-offs | Daily max → 85% back-offs |
| Back Squat | 1 × 5 | 85–90% |
| Front Squat | 3 × 3 | 80–85% |
| Clean Pull | 3 × 3 | 105–110% |

## Key Rules

- **Squat first, lift second:** Never let the back squat stop progressing. If the competition lifts plateau while squats are still improving, the problem is technique. If squats plateau too, the strength base needs to be rebuilt.
- **Dead-stop rows every time:** The Pendlay Row is performed from the floor — no momentum, no stretch reflex. Each rep starts from a dead stop. This is the structural foundation of the upper back that protects intermediate lifters from spinal injury under heavy pulls.
- **Recovery Day is not optional:** The Wednesday session exists to allow fatigue clearance. Training it too hard turns the three-stress structure into a grind and removes the intensity expression on Friday.

> After this program, see `olympic_weightlifting_advanced` or `cal_strength_daily` for higher-frequency daily maximum training.

# Dmitry Klokov Seminar Protocol

Dmitry Klokov, 2005 World Champion and 2008 Olympic silver medalist, developed a training methodology derived from Russian national team practices but adapted for his seminar curriculum. The system's central premise is that **technical proficiency is directly limited by positional strength**: an athlete cannot receive the bar correctly unless the muscles controlling the receiving position are strong enough to maintain it under load.

Klokov's program is characterized by:

1. **Strength-dominant accessory selection** — the Klokov Press (wide-grip behind-neck press), heavy trap work ("Trapi"), and Sots Press are used to build structural density in the upper back and shoulder girdle that no amount of competition lift practice can develop.
2. **Paused squat complexes** — back and front squat are combined in continuous sets with 3-second pauses to eliminate stretch reflex and build true positional strength at the bottom.
3. **Complex-heavy competition lift patterns** — rather than simple singles, Klokov chains exercises (muscle snatch + high-hang + floor) to accumulate technical volume while developing mechanical coordination across the full movement.
4. **Autoregulation** — on low-recovery days, intensity drops and volume shifts to accessory and positional work rather than forcing competition lift PRs.

## Who This Is For

- Advanced competitive lifters seeking to address structural deficiencies in the shoulder girdle or upper back
- Athletes whose snatch is limited by shoulder instability in the receiving position rather than pull strength
- Athletes who have completed a structured block periodization program and are looking for a high-specificity accessory framework
- Not appropriate for: athletes with shoulder impingement, thoracic outlet syndrome, or limited shoulder external rotation

## Prerequisite Mobility

The Klokov Press (behind-neck wide-grip press) requires:
- Full shoulder external rotation in the snatch grip width
- Complete thoracic extension under load
- Absence of shoulder impingement or anterior capsule tightness

Do not attempt the Klokov Press without confirming these prerequisites. The movement is contraindicated for athletes with any shoulder pathology.

## Sample Weekly Structure

| Day | AM Session | PM Session (if double) |
|-----|-----------|----------------------|
| Monday | Snatch complex; Klokov Press + OHS; Trapi | Front squat paused |
| Tuesday | Clean complex; Back + Front squat paused | Klokov Press; Trapi |
| Wednesday | Snatch complex (lighter); Sots Press | Rest |
| Thursday | Clean & Jerk; Back + Front squat paused | Klokov Press |
| Friday | Snatch complex; Klokov Press + OHS | Front squat |
| Saturday | Clean complex; Trapi; Sots Press | — |

## The Klokov Press — Technical Standard

- Grip width: snatch grip (index finger at ring marking or wider)
- Bar starts behind the neck at the base of the occiput
- Press strictly vertical — no forward lean, no hip extension assist
- At the top, elbows fully locked; bar directly over the mid-foot
- Descent: slow and controlled, full bar contact with upper traps at the bottom

**Any forward head position, thoracic rounding, or incomplete lockout means reducing load.**

## Key Rules

- **Trapi is not optional.** The upper trap work is the structural foundation of Klokov's system. Skipping it to save time removes the primary adaptation driver for shoulder girdle stability.
- **Autoregulate aggressively.** On days when systemic fatigue is elevated, drop to hang variations and accessory volume. Never force competition lift PRs through fatigue.
- **Positional strength before load.** The paused squat complexes are done at the heaviest load permitting full pause quality — never compromise the pause to add weight.

> For structured block periodization at this level, see `olympic_weightlifting_advanced`. For the ultra-high-frequency daily max approach, see `bulgarian_method`.

# LSU Shreveport 10-5-3 Program

The LSU Shreveport (LSUS) 10-5-3 Program was developed through collaboration between sports physiologist Dr. Michael Stone and USA Weightlifting Hall of Fame coach Dr. Kyle Pierce. It stands apart from other Olympic lifting programs through its conviction that **absolute force production is the primary ceiling of weightlifting performance**: once technique is established, the limiting factor is the athlete's ability to generate force, and that force production must be systematically maximized through structured hypertrophy and strength phases.

The program applies classic **linear block periodization** to a 5-day weekly structure:
- **Push Days (Mon/Wed):** back squat, push press, bench press, lunges — absolute strength and structural development
- **Pull Days (Tue/Thu):** snatch and clean derivatives, pulls, pull-ups — posterior chain and pull mechanics
- **Saturday:** full competition lifts from the floor

## Who This Is For

- Intermediate weightlifters whose competition lift progress has stalled due to strength deficits (not technique deficits)
- Athletes who can comfortably train 5 days/week and have exceptional recovery capacity (sleep, nutrition)
- Athletes with solid technique in both competition lifts who are ready for higher absolute volumes
- Not appropriate for: technique-limited athletes who have not yet established consistent receiving positions

## Weekly Schedule

| Day | Session | Primary Lifts |
|-----|---------|---------------|
| Monday | Push A | Back Squat (RM), Push Press, Bench Press |
| Tuesday | Pull A | Snatch Pull, Clean Pull, Pulls, Pull-ups |
| Wednesday | Push B | Back Squat (RM), Barbell Lunge, Push Press, Bench Press |
| Thursday | Pull B | Front Squat, Snatch Pull, Clean Pull, Pull-ups |
| Saturday | Olympic | Snatch, Clean & Jerk (full lifts, velocity sets) |

Friday and Sunday are mandatory rest.

## Block Structure and Rep Scheme

| Block | Weeks | Strength Lifts (Squat/Press) | Competition Derivatives |
|-------|-------|------------------------------|------------------------|
| Hypertrophy | 1–3 | Work to 10RM + 2 back-off sets | Snatch: 5 × 3; C&J: 5 × 2 |
| Strength | 4–6 | Work to 5RM + 2 back-off sets | Snatch: 4 × 2; C&J: 4 × 2 |
| Absolute Strength | 7–9 | Work to 3RM + 2 back-off sets | Snatch: 3 × 1; C&J: 3 × 1 |
| Taper | 10–11 | 60–70% volume, maintain intensity | Light technical work |
| Test Week | 12 | — | 1RM snatch + 1RM C&J |

## Back-Off Set Protocol

After every strength RM attempt (squat, press, bench):
1. First back-off set: 10% below the achieved RM weight, same rep target
2. Second back-off set: 5% below the achieved RM weight, same rep target

If the RM is not achieved (rep target missed), the back-off sets are still completed at the planned weights. Record the actual reps achieved.

## Monday — Push A

| Exercise | 10s Block | 5s Block | 3s Block |
|----------|-----------|----------|----------|
| Back Squat | 10RM + 2 back-offs | 5RM + 2 back-offs | 3RM + 2 back-offs |
| Push Press | 4 × 5 | 4 × 5 | 4 × 4 |
| Bench Press | 3 × 10 | 3 × 5 | 3 × 3 |

## Tuesday — Pull A

| Exercise | All Blocks |
|----------|------------|
| Snatch Pull | 4 × 3 @100–115% snatch 1RM (progresses by block) |
| Clean Pull | 4 × 3 @100–120% clean 1RM (progresses by block) |
| Pull-ups | 3 × max reps |

## Wednesday — Push B

| Exercise | 10s Block | 5s Block | 3s Block |
|----------|-----------|----------|----------|
| Back Squat | 10RM + 2 back-offs | 5RM + 2 back-offs | 3RM + 2 back-offs |
| Barbell Lunge | 3 × 8 each leg | 3 × 6 each leg | 3 × 5 each leg |
| Push Press | 3 × 5 | 3 × 5 | 3 × 4 |
| Bench Press | 3 × 8 | 3 × 5 | 3 × 3 |

## Thursday — Pull B

| Exercise | All Blocks |
|----------|------------|
| Front Squat | 3 × 3 @80–85% front squat 1RM |
| Snatch Pull | 4 × 3 @100–115% |
| Clean Pull | 4 × 3 @100–120% |
| Pull-ups | 3 × max reps |

## Saturday — Olympic Lifts

| Exercise | 10s Block | 5s Block | 3s Block |
|----------|-----------|----------|----------|
| Snatch | 5 × 3 @78–85% | 4 × 2 @83–90% | 3 × 1 @88–95% |
| Clean & Jerk | 5 × 2 @78–85% | 4 × 2 @83–90% | 3 × 1 @88–95% |

## Key Rules

- **RM attempts are true maxima.** The 10RM, 5RM, and 3RM are the heaviest weights achievable for those reps with good position — not a comfortable working weight. The system relies on progressive RM increments across weeks; if the RM doesn't move up each week, recovery or nutrition is insufficient.
- **Competition lifts preserve velocity.** The Olympic lifts use lower rep ranges in every block because bar velocity degrades irreparably above 3 reps per set in the snatch and C&J. Never apply the high-rep strength scheme to competition lifts.
- **Recovery requirements are non-negotiable.** The LSUS system carries massive systemic fatigue cost from its RM attempts and consecutive spinal loading days. Without 8+ hours of sleep and adequate caloric surplus, the program cannot be completed as written.

> For higher-frequency training at this level, see `glenn_pendlay_programs`. After this program, see `olympic_weightlifting_advanced`.

# Olympic Weightlifting Advanced — High-Frequency Competition Prep

Advanced Olympic weightlifting programming maximizes competition lift exposure. Unlike strength sports where the training tool (e.g., squats, pulls) can be separated from the competition expression, snatch and clean & jerk technique is so movement-specific that the primary adaptation mechanism is practicing the lifts themselves at near-maximal intensity. This program allocates six training days per week to competition lift practice and heavy squatting, with pulls and accessories serving as supplementary volume.

## Who This Is For

- Athletes competing in Olympic weightlifting at regional or national level
- Minimum baseline: Snatch ≥ 90% of body weight (male) or ≥ 80% (female); Clean & Jerk ≥ 110% of body weight (male) or ≥ 90% (female)
- Athletes with established technique on all major movements — this program volume assumes motor patterns are stable under heavy load

## 16-Week Block Structure

| Weeks | Phase | Character |
|-------|-------|-----------|
| 1–4 | Accumulation | Build volume; daily maxes at ~90% effort |
| 5–7 | Intensification | Push daily maxes higher; reduce accessory volume |
| 8 | Deload | 85% cap; no PRs |
| 9–12 | Intensification II | Heavy singles above previous bests |
| 13 | Deload | 85% cap |
| 14–15 | Peaking | Competition-openers and second attempts only |
| 16 | Competition Week | Monday: 90% and 95%. Tue–Thu: rest. Competition day. |

## Weekly Schedule

| Day | AM | PM |
|-----|----|----|
| Mon | Snatch + Back Squat | Snatch Pull + Power Clean |
| Tue | Clean & Jerk + Front Squat | Clean Pull + Push Press |
| Wed | Power Snatch + Back Squat | Overhead Squat + Good Morning |
| Thu | Snatch + Front Squat | Clean Pull + Power Clean |
| Fri | Clean & Jerk + Back Squat | Snatch Pull + Push Press |
| Sat | Power Snatch + Power Clean + Front Squat | Good Morning |
| Sun | Rest | |

Two-a-day sessions are separated by 4–6 hours minimum. PM sessions are shorter and lower intensity than AM sessions.

## Session Structure (AM — Competition Lift Day)

1. General warm-up: 10 min light rowing or bike
2. Movement warm-up: 3–4 sets with empty bar → 50% → 70% → 80%
3. Work to daily max (typically 3–5 progressive singles)
4. Back-off sets: 2–3 singles at 80–85% of daily max
5. Supplementary compound (squat or pull)

## Competition Preparation

**Openers**: select at 90–92% of best in training for each lift.
**Second attempts**: 97–100% of personal best.
**Third attempts**: PR or conservative +1 kg over previous best.

Attempt selection is the most critical non-training factor in competition performance. Conservative openers minimize missed lifts and allow full expression of fitness on the platform.

> For beginner Olympic lifting programming, see `olympic_weightlifting_beginner`. For intermediate: `catalyst_12_week`. For nutrition in weight-class sport: `energy_balance`, `hydration`.

# Olympic Weightlifting Beginner Program

Olympic weightlifting has the steepest technique learning curve of any barbell sport. The snatch and clean & jerk require a precise coordination of triple extension, bar trajectory, and receiving position that takes months to develop. This program uses **power versions** of both lifts as primary learning tools — the power snatch and power clean have the same pull mechanics as the full lifts but eliminate the deep squat receiving position, allowing the trainee to focus on the extension and turnover phases without the complexity of catching at depth.

The 12-week program builds from pure power versions toward progressively lower receiving positions, with strength work (squats, pulls) developing in parallel.

## Who This Is For

- Trainees new to Olympic lifting with some general barbell experience (can safely squat and deadlift)
- Athletes from other strength sports transitioning into Olympic lifting
- Not appropriate for: complete beginners with no barbell background — establish basic squatting and hinging mechanics first

## Schedule (3 days/week)

| Day | Session |
|-----|---------|
| Monday | Session A |
| Wednesday | Session B |
| Friday | Session C |

At least one full rest day between sessions is mandatory. The Olympic lifts require complete neuromuscular freshness.

## Session A

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Notes |
|----------|-------------|-------|
| Power Snatch | 5 × 3 | Technique load; stop if form degrades |
| Overhead Squat | 4 × 4–5 | Start light; build mobility over weeks |
| Front Squat | 4 × 4–6 | Primary strength work |
| Snatch Pull | 4 × 4 | @80–90% power snatch weight |
| Back Squat | 3 × 5–8 | Strength base |

## Session B

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Notes |
|----------|-------------|-------|
| Power Clean | 5 × 3 | Technique load |
| Power Snatch | 3 × 3 | Lighter than Session A |
| Snatch Pull | 3 × 4 | |
| Clean Pull | 4 × 4 | @80–90% power clean weight |
| Romanian Deadlift | 3 × 6–8 | |

## Session C

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Notes |
|----------|-------------|-------|
| Power Snatch | 4 × 3 | |
| Power Clean | 4 × 3 | |
| Push Press | 4 × 4–5 | |
| Clean Pull | 3 × 4 | |
| Front Squat | 3 × 5 | |
| Back Squat | 3 × 5 | |

## Weeks 9–12: Introduction to Full Lifts

In the final four weeks, gradually replace power versions with squat versions once receiving positions are established:
- Replace power snatch with hang snatch (above knee) × 3
- Replace power clean with hang clean × 3
- Overhead squat should now be loaded at ≥ 60% of power snatch for stability

Do not force the transition to full lifts before receiving positions are structurally sound.

## Mobility Prerequisites

Before loading overhead squat or snatch:
- Wrist: 70° of dorsiflexion (test: hands flat on floor, lean forward)
- Shoulder: full elevation without lumbar compensation
- Ankle: adequate dorsiflexion for squat depth (see `mobility_requirements`)

Restricted mobility should be addressed before loading, not alongside heavy loading.

> For competition-level training after this program, see `catalyst_12_week`. For supporting nutrition see `energy_balance`.

# Olympic Weightlifting Intermediate Program

This 12-week program bridges the gap between learning the competition lifts and training toward genuine performance numbers. The defining challenge at the intermediate level is consistency: producing technically clean lifts at 85–95% of 1RM across multiple sessions per week, not just on good days. The program structures this around two types of sessions for each lift — one heavy day where the athlete works to a daily max, and one volume day where mechanics are reinforced at controlled intensities.

## Who This Is For

- Athletes who can perform the full snatch and clean & jerk with sound mechanics (no longer using power versions as primary movements)
- Snatch 1RM in the range of 60–90% bodyweight; C&J 1RM in the range of 75–110% bodyweight
- Has completed a beginner program or equivalent and is no longer making weekly PR improvements
- Not appropriate for: athletes still developing receiving position mechanics — complete the beginner program first

## Schedule (4 days/week)

| Day | Session | Primary Focus |
|-----|---------|---------------|
| Monday | Session A | Snatch heavy + Front Squat + Overhead Squat + Snatch Pull |
| Tuesday | Session B | C&J heavy + Back Squat + Clean Pull |
| Thursday | Session C | Power Snatch (diagnostic) + Snatch volume + Front Squat + Snatch Pull |
| Friday | Session D | C&J volume + Clean Pull + Front Squat |

Wednesday and the weekend are mandatory rest. The Tuesday-Thursday gap (one rest day between heavy and volume sessions) is intentional — neuromuscular readiness for volume work requires partial recovery from the heavy session.

## Session A — Snatch Heavy

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Intensity |
|----------|-------------|-----------|
| Overhead Squat | 3 × 3–4 | 60–70% snatch 1RM |
| Snatch | Build to daily max, then 2–3 back-off singles | Daily max; back-offs at 85% of daily max |
| Front Squat | 4 × 3 | 80–90% front squat 1RM |
| Snatch Pull | 4 × 3 | 95–105% snatch 1RM (Block 1 & 3); 110–120% (Block 2) |

Overhead squat opens every Session A — it is a positional check before loading the snatch, not a warm-up exercise. If overhead squat quality is noticeably lower than last week, reduce the snatch daily max target by 5%.

## Session B — Clean & Jerk Heavy

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Intensity |
|----------|-------------|-----------|
| Clean & Jerk | Build to daily max, then 2–3 back-off singles | Daily max; back-offs at 85% of daily max |
| Back Squat | 4 × 4–5 | 75–85% back squat 1RM |
| Clean Pull | 4 × 3 | 95–105% clean 1RM (Block 1 & 3); 110–120% (Block 2) |

## Session C — Snatch Volume

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Intensity |
|----------|-------------|-----------|
| Power Snatch | 3 × 3 | 75–80% snatch 1RM |
| Snatch | 5 × 2 | 78–85% snatch 1RM (block-dependent) |
| Hang Snatch (Block 2+) | 3 × 3 | 70–75% snatch 1RM |
| Front Squat | 3 × 4–5 | 70–80% front squat 1RM |
| Snatch Pull | 4 × 3 | 95–105% snatch 1RM (Block 1 & 3); 110–120% (Block 2) |

Power snatch is always performed before the full snatch. If bar path in the power snatch is inconsistent, reduce the full snatch volume sets from 5 × 2 to 3 × 2 and focus on position.

## Session D — C&J Volume

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Intensity |
|----------|-------------|-----------|
| Clean & Jerk | 4 × 1+1 | 80–88% C&J 1RM (block-dependent) |
| Clean Pull | 4 × 3 | 95–105% clean 1RM |
| Front Squat | 3 × 4–5 | 70–80% front squat 1RM |

The "1+1" notation means one clean followed by one jerk as a complex — not a split-second touch-and-jerk, but a deliberate pause in the rack position (3–5 seconds) before the jerk. This pause is training the rack reset and breath control that become critical at 90%+ loads.

## Block Structure

### Block 1 — Accumulation (Weeks 1–4)

Focus: technical consistency at moderate intensity; establish baseline daily max numbers.

| Session | Snatch/C&J working sets | Squats |
|---------|------------------------|--------|
| A / B (heavy) | Daily max target: 88–92% of 1RM | Front squat: 80–85% |
| C / D (volume) | 5 × 2 at 78–82% | Front squat: 70–75% |

Week 4 is a deload: all working weights reduced to 70–75%; daily max capped at 85% of 1RM; no attempts above this ceiling.

### Block 2 — Intensification (Weeks 5–8)

Focus: push daily max toward and occasionally past 1RM; introduce hang snatch in Session C. Pull intensity increases to 110–120% to maximize peak vertical ground reaction force and overload spinal extensors.

| Session | Snatch/C&J working sets | Squats |
|---------|------------------------|--------|
| A / B (heavy) | Daily max target: 92–97% of 1RM | Front squat: 85–90% |
| C / D (volume) | 5 × 2 at 82–86% | Front squat: 75–80% |
| Pulls (all sessions) | 4 × 3 at 110–120% of lift 1RM | — |

Begin hang snatch in Session C from Week 5. Update 1RM estimates based on best daily maxes from Block 1 before setting Block 2 percentages.

Week 8 is a deload: same protocol as Week 4.

### Block 3 — Peaking (Weeks 9–12)

Focus: consolidate heavy singles; attempt new 1RMs in week 11 or 12 test week.

| Session | Snatch/C&J working sets | Squats |
|---------|------------------------|--------|
| A / B (heavy) | Daily max target: 95–100%+ of 1RM | Front squat: 88–93% |
| C / D (volume) | Reduce to 3 × 2 at 82–86% | Front squat: 75–82% |

In Block 3, reduce volume day set count (3 × 2, not 5 × 2) to allow full recovery for heavy days. Week 12 can be structured as a mini-competition: attempt a new 1RM in snatch and C&J on separate days (snatch on Monday, C&J on Tuesday or Wednesday).

## Key Rules

- **RPE 9.5 ceiling on daily max:** The daily max is bounded by feel, not by a target number. Never attempt a weight that leaves less than 1 rep in reserve — if the last successful rep required grinding or significant positional breakdown, that is the daily max. True failure is prohibited.
- **Two-miss rule:** After two consecutive misses at the same weight, reduce by 3–5 kg and complete one successful lift before stopping that movement. Never end a session on a failed lift.
- **Back-off sets are mandatory:** After every daily max attempt, complete 2–3 back-off singles at 85% of the daily max reached. Skipping back-offs to protect the feeling of a heavy single is the most common error at this level.
- **Front squat priority:** If time or energy requires cutting one exercise, it is never front squat. Back squat, pulls, and accessory work are cut first.
- **Power snatch diagnostic:** If power snatch percentage of squat snatch exceeds 88%, the athlete is power-dependent and catch mechanics need dedicated attention before loads increase further.

> For the meta-knowledge framework governing load decisions, session structure, and advancement signals, see `olympic_intermediate_guide`.
> After this program, see `olympic_weightlifting_advanced` for daily-max high-frequency training.

# USAW Level 1 Coaching Framework

The USA Weightlifting Level 1 framework is the standard entry point for structured Olympic lifting in the United States. Its defining feature is the **top-down technical progression**: athletes learn each lift starting from the high-hang position — the simplest catch mechanic — and progressively move the starting position toward the floor as positional competency is established. This approach prevents the accumulation of technique flaws from floor-up teaching, where the first pull is learned independently of the catch position.

The program uses **power versions** throughout the 12 weeks. The power snatch and power clean maintain identical pull mechanics to the full lifts while eliminating the depth requirement, allowing the coach and athlete to focus entirely on pull quality, extension timing, and receiving mechanics.

## Who This Is For

- Athletes completely new to Olympic lifting
- Trainees with some general barbell experience (can safely hinge and squat)
- Athletes returning after a long break who need a technique reset
- Not appropriate for: athletes who already have stable squat receiving positions

## Prerequisite Screening

Before loading the overhead squat or snatch:

| Mobility Check | Standard |
|---------------|----------|
| Wrist dorsiflexion | Flat palms on floor, arms straight |
| Shoulder elevation | Full overhead reach without lumbar compensation |
| Ankle dorsiflexion | Squat below parallel with flat foot |
| Thoracic extension | Upright torso in overhead squat at bodyweight |

Address mobility restrictions before loading, not alongside heavy loading. See `mobility_requirements`.

## Schedule (3 days/week)

| Day | Session |
|-----|---------|
| Monday | Session A |
| Wednesday | Session B |
| Friday | Session C |

## Session A

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Notes |
|----------|-------------|-------|
| Power Snatch | 4 × 3 | Top-down; advance position when stable |
| Power Clean | 4 × 3 | Top-down |
| Snatch Pull | 4 × 4 | @85–95% power snatch |
| Front Squat | 4 × 4–6 | Position before load |
| Back Squat | 3 × 5 | Strength base |

## Session B

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Notes |
|----------|-------------|-------|
| Power Snatch | 4 × 3 | |
| Power Clean | 4 × 3 | |
| Snatch Pull | 3 × 4 | |
| Clean Pull | 4 × 4 | @85–95% power clean |
| Overhead Squat | 3 × 4–5 | Start with PVC or empty bar |
| Push Press | 3 × 5 | Vertical dip |

## Session C

| Exercise | Sets × Reps | Notes |
|----------|-------------|-------|
| Power Snatch | 4 × 3 | |
| Power Clean | 4 × 3 | |
| Overhead Squat | 3 × 4–5 | |
| Push Press | 3 × 5 | |
| Clean Pull | 3 × 4 | |
| Front Squat | 3 × 5 | |
| Back Squat | 3 × 5 | |

## Top-Down Position Progression

| Weeks | Snatch Starting Position | Clean Starting Position |
|-------|--------------------------|-------------------------|
| 1–3 | High-hang power snatch | High-hang power clean |
| 4–6 | Hang power snatch (above knee) | Hang power clean (above knee) |
| 7–9 | Hang power snatch (at knee) | Hang power clean (at knee) |
| 10–12 | Power snatch from floor | Power clean from floor |

**Advancement criterion:** Technically stable position AND consistent bar path across two consecutive sessions before advancing.

## Key Rules

- **Position before load:** Never increase weight when the receiving position is inconsistent. The bar waits; the position doesn't.
- **Session time cap:** 60–75 minutes. Exceeded regularly → reduce volume before adding load.
- **Squat versions are not introduced in this program.** After Week 12, transition to `olympic_weightlifting_beginner` for squat version introduction.

> After this program, see `olympic_weightlifting_beginner`. For supporting mobility work, see `mobility_requirements`.
