# 5/3/1

5/3/1 is a percentage-based barbell strength program built around four main lifts — squat, bench press, overhead press, and deadlift — each trained once per week. The central philosophy is deliberate underloading: all working percentages are calculated from a **training max** (TM) set to roughly 90% of true 1RM, keeping most sessions comfortably below the athlete's actual limit. Progress is slow by design — small, reliable monthly increases compound into large long-term gains without the burnout and stalls that come from chasing true maxes every week.

## How the Training Max Works

The training max is not your 1RM. Set it to approximately **90% of your current true 1RM**, or — if you are new to the program — use a conservative estimate (a weight you could lift for 3–5 reps with technique intact). All week-by-week percentages in the program are calculated from this TM, not your true 1RM. This means working sets feel moderate early in a cycle, which is intentional. The cumulative fatigue over weeks 1–3 is what drives adaptation; no single session should feel like a true max effort until the AMRAP set on week 3.

## The Four-Week Wave

Each lift runs through the same 4-week wave. Percentages are of the **training max**:

| Week | Set 1 | Set 2 | Set 3 |
|------|-------|-------|-------|
| 1 | 65% × 5 | 75% × 5 | 85% × **5+** |
| 2 | 70% × 3 | 80% × 3 | 90% × **3+** |
| 3 | 75% × 5 | 85% × 3 | 95% × **1+** |
| 4 (deload) | 40% × 5 | 50% × 5 | 60% × 5 |

The `+` notation on the final set means **AMRAP** (as many reps as possible). Do not sandbag the AMRAP — it is the primary progress signal and auto-regulation mechanism of the program.

## AMRAP Sets

The AMRAP set on weeks 1–3 serves two purposes. First, it creates overload beyond what the prescribed rep target alone would provide — if you're capable of 12 reps at 85% TM (week 1), you should do 12. Second, the rep count becomes a real-time e1RM estimate: a high AMRAP indicates the TM is conservative and monthly increases are sustainable; a barely-made AMRAP suggests you are near the TM ceiling and a reset may be approaching.

Do not end the AMRAP at a fixed number to "save something." Leave nothing on the bar (while maintaining form).

## Progression

After completing each 4-week cycle, increase the training max:

- **Upper body lifts** (bench press, overhead press): **+2.5 kg**
- **Lower body lifts** (squat, deadlift): **+5 kg**

These increases are applied to the TM, not the true 1RM. Repeat the wave with the new TM.

**When to reset:** If AMRAP reps on the final set stall or decline across two consecutive cycles for the same lift, do not keep adding weight. Reset that lift's TM to **85–90% of the current TM** and rebuild from there. Resets are not failures — they are mandatory maintenance that keeps the program running for years.

## Assistance Work

The four main-lift sets are the core of each session. Wendler prescribes assistance templates to build muscle and address weaknesses around the main lifts. The most common is **Boring But Big (BBB)**: after the main lift sets, perform 5 × 10 of the same lift (or a variation) at 50–60% TM. This adds significant volume without compromising recovery for the main sets.

Other common templates:
- **First Set Last (FSL)**: After the main wave, do multiple sets at the first-set percentage (e.g., 65% on week 1). Less volume than BBB, better for busier schedules.
- **Triumvirate**: Three assistance exercises per session — one push, one pull, one single-leg/core — for 3–5 sets each.

Assistance work is **not optional**, but it is secondary. If recovery is compromised, cut assistance first, never main lift volume.

## Why It Works

The submaximal training max ensures that the vast majority of sessions stay well clear of true failure. This limits accumulated fatigue, keeps technique cleaner under load, and makes the small monthly increases sustainable for years rather than weeks. Most lifters on aggressive linear progression stall within 3–6 months; 5/3/1 athletes regularly run productive cycles for 2–3 years before needing a major programming change.

The AMRAP set provides the intensity stimulus that simple submaximal training would lack, and the deload week provides the fatigue dissipation that allows the next cycle to start fresh. The result is a low-complexity, high-durability system that produces consistent strength gains without daily decision-making.

# GZCLP

Created by powerlifter Cody LeClair. A four-day linear progression program using three tiers of diminishing specificity and increasing volume. Tolerates more training stimulus than Starting Strength while remaining appropriate for beginners and early-intermediate lifters.

## Design Philosophy — Why Three Tiers

GZCLP is built around Cody LeClair's observation that simple 5×5 programs (Starting Strength, StrongLifts) produce diminishing returns after 2–3 months because they only train one quality: heavy low-rep strength. The tier system solves this by training three qualities simultaneously, each with its own progression:

**Tier 1 (heavy, low-rep)** trains maximal strength through the competition lifts at 5×3. This is the same stimulus as Starting Strength, but at slightly lower reps per set — which means heavier loads for the same number of total reps. The AMRAP last set provides autoregulation: if you crush the set, your training max is probably conservative; if you barely get 3, it's well-calibrated.

**Tier 2 (moderate, moderate-rep)** trains the same movement pattern at 3×10 with lighter weight. This accumulates hypertrophy volume that a pure strength program lacks. More muscle cross-sectional area means a higher strength ceiling later. T2 progresses independently of T1, so you can keep building muscle even when your heavy strength stalls.

**Tier 3 (light, high-rep)** is isolation work for weak points and joint health. It produces the least fatigue per set and addresses imbalances that compound lifts miss.

**Why the failure cascade matters:** When T1 stalls at 5×3, you don't deload — you change the rep scheme to 6×2, then 10×1. This extends the linear progression by reducing reps per set while increasing total sets, so you can continue adding weight by reducing per-set difficulty. Only after exhausting all three rep schemes do you reset. This gives beginners 3–4 months of additional progression compared to programs that deload at the first stall.

## Four-Day Schedule

| Day | T1 — 5×3+ | T2 — 4×10 | T3 — 3×15+ |
|-----|-----------|-----------|------------|
| 1 | Back Squat | Bench Press | Pull-ups |
| 2 | OHP | Deadlift | Pull-ups |
| 3 | Deadlift | Back Squat | Pull-ups |
| 4 | Bench Press | OHP | Pull-ups |

## Tier Definitions

| Tier | Sets × Reps | Purpose | Progression trigger |
|------|-------------|---------|---------------------|
| T1 | 5×3 + AMRAP | Specificity — competition lift strength | Every session: +5 kg lower / +2.5 kg upper |
| T2 | 4×10 | Supplemental volume at sub-maximal load | All 40 reps completed cleanly |
| T3 | 3×15+ AMRAP | Accessories / GPP | ≥ 25 total reps across 3 sets |

## T1 Failure Cascade

| Failure | Response |
|---------|----------|
| 1st fail | Switch to 6×2 at same weight next session |
| 2nd fail | Switch to 10×1 at same weight |
| 3rd fail | Reset load −15%, restart at 5×3+ |

## Common T3 Accessories

| Session slot | Examples |
|--------------|---------|
| Upper T3 | Pull-ups, dips, face pulls, triceps pushdown |
| Lower T3 | Leg press, lying leg curl, calf raise, ab wheel |

> Beginner alternative to `starting_strength` with higher volume tolerance. Transition to `texas_method` or full GZCL block when linear gains end.

# Starting Strength

Starting Strength is a novice barbell strength program built around five foundational
movements — the low bar back squat, the overhead press, the conventional deadlift, the
bench press, and the power clean. It alternates two workouts (A and B) performed three
times per week with a rest day between each session. The central mechanism is
**novice linear progression**: add weight every session, because a novice's recovery
capacity is fast enough to supercompensate between sessions.

## Program Structure

**Workout A**: Back Squat 3×5 / Overhead Press 3×5 / Deadlift 1×5

**Workout B**: Back Squat 3×5 / Bench Press 3×5 / Power Clean 5×3

The squat appears in every session because it is the primary strength developer. The
deadlift is performed for only one working set — not three — because a single heavy set
at novice loads produces adequate stimulus; additional sets would impair recovery for the
next session.

## Training Max and Starting Weight

Starting Strength does not use a training max or percentage-based programming. Start with
an empty bar (or the lightest manageable load with technique intact) and add weight every
session. The correct starting weight is a weight that feels easy today — the linear
progression makes it challenging within 2–3 weeks. Starting too heavy is the most common
error; it guarantees an early stall.

## Progression

- **Squat / Deadlift**: +5 kg (10 lbs) per session
- **Press / Bench Press**: +2.5 kg (5 lbs) per session
- **Power Clean**: +2.5 kg (5 lbs) per session

If a lift fails (cannot complete all prescribed reps) on one session, retry the same
weight next session. If it fails a second time, perform a 10% weight reduction on that
lift and rebuild. Frequent stalls across multiple lifts signal the end of the novice
phase; transition to an intermediate program such as 5/3/1.

## Why the Low Bar Back Squat

Rippetoe prescribes the low bar position specifically. Placing the bar below the spine
of the scapula shortens the moment arm between the bar and the hip joint, forcing a more
horizontal torso and shifting more load to the posterior chain (hamstrings, glutes,
adductors). The result is a more mechanically efficient squat for raw strength because
it recruits the largest muscles of the body.

## Why One Deadlift Set

The deadlift is 1 working set of 5, not 3. The deadlift engages the entire posterior
chain already taxed by the preceding squats. At novice loads, one heavy set drives
adaptation; three sets creates excessive systemic fatigue that compromises recovery
and frequency.

## When to Leave the Program

The novice phase ends when consistent session-to-session weight additions are no longer
possible despite adequate sleep, nutrition, and technique — typically 3–6 months in.
Transition to a program with weekly or monthly progression cadence.

# Texas Method

Designed by Mark Rippetoe as the intermediate step after novice linear progression. Three days per week with distinct physiological roles: Volume, Recovery, Intensity.

## Design Philosophy — Why Volume/Recovery/Intensity

The Texas Method exists to solve the specific problem that ends beginner programs: when you can no longer add weight every session, but you can still add weight every week.

**Monday (Volume)** accumulates training stress through 5×5 at a moderate percentage. This is the stimulus day — high total volume drives the adaptation signal. The 5×5 format was chosen because it produces sufficient mechanical tension and metabolic stress without requiring loads so heavy that technique breaks down.

**Wednesday (Recovery)** uses lighter loads (80% of Monday's weight) at lower volume (2×5). This session maintains movement pattern practice and promotes active recovery without adding meaningful fatigue. Removing Wednesday entirely was tested and found to slightly impair Friday performance — the light stimulus helps maintain neural readiness.

**Friday (Intensity)** tests the adaptation with a single heavy set of 5 at a new personal record. This is where progression is measured: if you hit 5 reps, add weight next week. The weekly PR attempt is the core mechanism — it converts Monday's volume stimulus into demonstrated strength.

**Why this works when daily progression doesn't:** A novice can recover from a heavy session in 48–72 hours and set a new PR. An intermediate's SRA curve (see `sra_curve`) is longer — they need an entire week of stimulus + recovery to produce the same adaptation. The Texas Method structures that weekly cycle explicitly: stress → recover → express.

## Weekly Structure

| Day | Role | Squat | Press | Pull |
|-----|------|-------|-------|------|
| Monday | Volume | 5×5 @ ~90% 5RM | 5×5 bench or OHP | — |
| Wednesday | Recovery | 2×5 @ ~80% 5RM | 3×5 (opposite lift, light) | — |
| Friday | Intensity | 1×5 PR | 1×5 PR (same as Mon) | 1×5 deadlift PR |

## Press Alternation (A/B Weeks)

| Week | Volume + Intensity | Recovery |
|------|--------------------|----------|
| A | Bench Press | OHP 3×5 |
| B | Overhead Press | Bench Press 3×5 |

## Common Assistance

- Pull-ups / chin-ups: 3 sets on Volume Day after pressing
- Back extensions or good mornings: 3×10 on Intensity Day after deadlift
- Dips: Recovery Day triceps volume

## Progression Rules

1. Add 2.5 kg to upper lifts, 5 kg to squat/deadlift on Intensity Day each week
2. Two consecutive failed PRs = stall → adjust Volume Day or deload 10%
3. Volume Day should feel challenging but submaximal; if 5×5 is impossible, reduce load 5%

> Natural progression from `starting_strength`. When TM stalls, transition to `5_3_1` or block periodization.

# Westside Conjugate Method

The Westside Conjugate Method is a concurrent periodization system developed by Louie
Simmons that trains maximal strength and explosive speed-strength simultaneously rather
than in sequential phases. It operates on a four-day weekly split: two **Max Effort (ME)**
days develop absolute strength through heavy singles and triples; two **Dynamic Effort
(DE)** days develop rate of force development through submaximal loads performed with
maximum bar speed.

The method is named "conjugate" because it conjugates (joins together) multiple physical
qualities — strength, speed-strength, and repetition-method volume — within the same
training week, rather than isolating each quality in separate mesocycles.

## Four-Day Structure

| Day | Method | Focus | Primary exercise |
|-----|--------|-------|-----------------|
| Monday | Max Effort (ME) | Lower | Rotating heavy squat/deadlift variation |
| Wednesday | Max Effort (ME) | Upper | Rotating heavy press variation |
| Friday | Dynamic Effort (DE) | Lower | Box squat (fixed) + deadlift work |
| Saturday | Dynamic Effort (DE) | Upper | Bench press with bands/chains (fixed) |

The 72-hour separation between ME and DE days for the same movement pattern is mandatory.
Training the same quality twice within 72 hours for the same pattern prevents full CNS
recovery.

## Max Effort Method

On ME days, work up to a **1–3 repetition maximum** on the primary exercise. This is not
a heavy training set — it is a genuine maximal effort. The critical rule: **rotate the
primary ME exercise every session**. Using the same movement on consecutive ME days
allows the CNS to adapt and reduces the training effect. Any variation should not repeat
until at least 3 weeks have passed.

**Rotating ME Lower exercises** (examples):
- Conventional deadlift (1–3RM)
- Sumo deadlift (1–3RM)
- Good morning (1–3RM)
- Deadlift from deficit (1–3RM)
- Box squat to parallel or below (1RM)

**Rotating ME Upper exercises** (examples):
- 2-board press (most common)
- 3-board press
- Floor press
- Close grip bench press
- Bench press with chains

After the primary ME exercise, perform 3–5 accessory exercises targeting weak muscle
groups (3–5 sets of 5–8 reps each). Common pairings: posterior chain work (back
extensions, glute-ham raises) on ME lower days; triceps and upper back work (close grip
presses, face pulls, rows) on ME upper days.

## Dynamic Effort Method

On DE days, the primary exercise is **fixed** — not rotated. Box squat on DE lower;
bench press on DE upper. The variable is load, which runs on a **3-week pendulum wave**.

### DE Lower — Box Squat Pendulum Wave

| Wave Week | Bar weight | Accommodating resistance | Sets × Reps | Rest |
|-----------|-----------|--------------------------|-------------|------|
| Week 1 | 50% 1RM | ~25% via bands or chains | 12 × 2 | 45–60 s |
| Week 2 | 55% 1RM | ~25% via bands or chains | 12 × 2 | 45–60 s |
| Week 3 | 60% 1RM | ~25% via bands or chains | 10 × 2 | 45–60 s |

After week 3, reset to week 1. After the box squat sets, perform 6–10 supplemental
deadlift sets at submaximal load (conventional or sumo, chosen based on competition
stance).

### DE Upper — Bench Press Pendulum Wave

| Wave Week | Bar weight | Accommodating resistance | Sets × Reps | Rest |
|-----------|-----------|--------------------------|-------------|------|
| Week 1 | 50% 1RM | ~25% via bands | 9 × 3 | 45–60 s |
| Week 2 | 55% 1RM | ~25% via bands | 9 × 3 | 45–60 s |
| Week 3 | 60% 1RM | ~25% via bands | 9 × 3 | 45–60 s |

Rotate grip width across the 9 bench sets (3 close / 3 medium / 3 wide) to distribute
the load across the pec, front delt, and triceps.

## Why Accommodating Resistance

A straight barbell provides a fixed load throughout the range of motion, but a lifter's
strength curve rises toward lockout. Bands or chains add load progressively through the
range of motion, matching the ascending strength curve. This creates more uniform muscle
activation and a greater training stimulus at lockout than straight weight alone.
Bands contribute approximately 25% of the total load at lockout.

## Who Is This Program For

The Westside Conjugate Method is an **advanced** program. It requires:
- High technical proficiency in the competition lifts and their variations
- Access to specialty bars, bands, and chains
- Experience reading daily readiness to regulate ME effort
- Familiarity with the ME exercise rotation to manage variation systematically

Intermediate lifters are better served by simpler linear or undulating programs. The
complexity and equipment demands produce less additional benefit for lifters who would
progress on simpler programming.

# Basic Strongman Block

An 8-week introductory strongman training block combining barbell strength work with event-specific training. Designed for athletes with a strength training base (e.g., 1.5x bodyweight deadlift) entering their first competition.

## 8-Week Block Structure

| Phase | Weeks | Focus |
|-------|-------|-------|
| Accumulation | 1-4 | Volume, technique, 65-75% 1RM |
| Intensification | 5-7 | Heavy singles/triples, 80-90% 1RM |
| Peak / Taper | 8 | Competition weights, reduced volume |

## Weekly Schedule

| Day | Session |
|-----|---------|
| Monday | Lower - Deadlift + Yoke / Farmer carry |
| Tuesday | Upper - Log / Axle press + Upper back |
| Thursday | Lower - Squat + Sled / Tire |
| Saturday | Event day - Atlas stones, Keg load, Car deadlift simulator |

## Day 1 - Deadlift + Carries (Accumulation Example)

| Exercise | Sets x Reps | Notes |
|----------|-------------|-------|
| Conventional Deadlift | 4 x 5 | 70-80% 1RM |
| Deficit Deadlift | 3 x 3 | 60-70% 1RM |
| Yoke Carry | 4 x 20 m | Moderate weight, focus on speed |
| Farmer Carry | 4 x 20 m | Per hand, build to competition weight |
| Leg Press | 3 x 10 | Quad accessory |

## Day 2 - Log Press + Upper (Accumulation Example)

| Exercise | Sets x Reps | Notes |
|----------|-------------|-------|
| Log Clean and Press | 5 x 5 | Push-press allowed |
| Seated Dumbbell Press | 3 x 10-12 | Shoulder hypertrophy |
| Bent Over Row | 4 x 8 | Upper back base |
| Kroc Row | 3 x 15/arm | Lat and grip |
| Face Pull | 3 x 15 | Rear delt and rotator cuff |

## Day 4 - Squat + Sled (Accumulation Example)

| Exercise | Sets x Reps | Notes |
|----------|-------------|-------|
| Back Squat | 4 x 5 | 70-75% 1RM |
| Front Squat | 3 x 3 | Yoke and stone pickup prep |
| Sled Push | 4 x 20 m | Heavy; sprint for conditioning |
| Tire Flip | 4 x 5 flips | Hip extension power |
| Leg Curl | 3 x 10-12 | Hamstring accessory |

## Day 5 - Event Day (Rotating)

Rotate through competition events:
- **Atlas stones:** 3-5 singles to platform or lap-and-load
- **Keg load:** 3-5 loads over bar at competition height
- **Car deadlift / frame:** Multiple singles at competition weight
- **Medley:** Farmer + yoke + keg combination

## Key Implement Progressions

| Event | Accumulation | Intensification | Peak |
|-------|-------------|-----------------|------|
| Yoke | 75% comp weight, 20 m | 90% comp weight, 15 m | Competition weight, 10 m |
| Farmer | 60-70% comp/hand | 80-90% comp/hand | Competition weight |
| Log Press | 5x5 at 70% | 3x3 at 85% | 1-2 heavy singles |
| Atlas Stone | Technique reps | Timed loads | Competition stone weight |

## Grip and Conditioning

- Wrist wraps only when necessary; train grip without straps on most sets
- Loaded carry conditioning replaces traditional cardio
- Chalk always available; straps for deadlift max-effort only

> For barbell strength base before strongman, see `texas_method` or `5_3_1`. For Olympic lifting power development, see `catalyst_12_week`.

# Brian Shaw Off-Season Training Structure

Brian Shaw is a four-time World's Strongest Man champion (2011, 2013, 2015, 2016) and one of the most documented professional strongman athletes in the sport's history. His off-season training philosophy represents a mature professional framework that prioritizes **absolute strength accumulation at sub-maximal event specificity**: the off-season exists to build a higher strength ceiling for the next competition prep block, not to simulate competitions.

Shaw's system is conjugate-inspired in that it separates maximum effort work from dynamic/volume work across the week, but it does not follow strict Westside percentages or rotation protocols. The system is based on documented training logs from his YouTube channel and competition preparation content from 2011–2020.

The central principle is **progressive overload on absolute strength movements across a full off-season macrocycle (typically 6–8 months between competition cycles)**. Maximum effort days push the training ceiling; volume days accumulate structural hypertrophy and posterior-chain development. Event work is maintenance-level only — enough to preserve the neural patterns, not enough to generate competition-level fatigue.

## Who This Is For

- Elite advanced strongman athletes competing at national or international level
- Athletes in the off-season period between two competition cycles (typically 6–8 months)
- Athletes who can sustain 4 days/week of high-intensity training with professional recovery support
- Not appropriate for: athletes in competition prep, intermediate athletes without established event proficiency

## Weekly Schedule

| Day | Focus |
|-----|-------|
| Monday | ME Deadlift + ME Squat |
| Tuesday | ME Overhead + Upper back / Trap work |
| Wednesday | Accessory (good morning, posterior chain) |
| Thursday | Volume Squat + Events (stones, yoke, tire) |
| Friday | Volume Overhead + Event (log, farmers) |

## Maximum Effort (ME) Day Structure

| Component | Protocol |
|-----------|----------|
| ME Movement | Work up to heavy 3–5 reps (not a true 1RM) |
| Back-off sets | 3 × 5 at 75–80% of day's max |
| Supplemental | 2–3 accessory movements at 3 × 8–12 |

## Key Rules

- **Off-season is not competition prep.** No event maxes, no taper, no intensity peaks. The purpose is strength accumulation, not performance expression. Attempting competition-weight event maxes in the off-season creates unnecessary injury risk without performance benefit.
- **Trap development is non-negotiable.** Heavy shrug volume is as important as the primary strength lifts. The upper trapezius is the primary structural limit of yoke and stone performance at the elite level.
- **Maximum effort work stops at 5 reps.** Working to a true 1RM in the off-season is unnecessary — a heavy 3–5 rep set generates sufficient neural demand while reducing injury risk from maximal loads on tired tissue.

> Transition to `strongman_competition_prep` when a specific contest date is identified (typically 16 weeks out).

# Cube Method Strongman

The Cube Method was developed by Josh Thigpen, a professional strongman competitor and powerlifter, as a systematic answer to the question: "How do you train maximum strength, explosive power, and volume in the same week without overtaxing any single quality?" The answer is rotation.

The Cube Method's core principle is that **each primary quality — maximal strength, explosive power, and volume — is trained on a 3-week rotation**, and **no two primary lifts share the same emphasis in the same week**. On any given training day, one lift is in the heavy phase while the others are in explosive or volume phases. This ensures that the CNS demand of maximum effort work never stacks across the weekly schedule.

The adaptation to strongman is notable: the event training follows the **same 3-week wave as the barbell lifts**, creating a parallel periodization structure. A heavy deadlift week coincides with an explosive event day; a volume squat week coincides with a heavy log day. The result is a systematic rotation through every stimulus type across all training modalities.

## Who This Is For

- Intermediate athletes with 1–3 years of strongman training
- Athletes competing at regional or national amateur level
- Athletes who have exhausted linear progression and need structured undulating periodization
- Athletes with consistent access to all competition implements
- Minimum baseline: Deadlift ≥ 2× BW, Overhead Press ≥ 1.0× BW, Squat ≥ 1.75× BW

## The Three-Week Cube Wave

| Week Type | Barbell Lifts | Event Training |
|-----------|--------------|---------------|
| Week A — Heavy | Work to 5RM / 3RM / 1RM (progresses each cycle) | Full event at 90–95% competition weight |
| Week B — Explosive | 6–8 × 2 at 60%; maximum bar speed | Event at 60–65%; start speed and acceleration focus |
| Week C — Volume | 5 × 5 at 70–75% | Event at 65–70%; maximum distance or loading volume |

The wave repeats four times across 12 weeks. Each repetition of the heavy week targets a slightly higher load.

## Four-Day Weekly Schedule

| Day | Focus |
|-----|-------|
| Monday | Deadlift (Cube wave) + Posterior chain |
| Wednesday | Squat (Cube wave, offset) + Events (stones) |
| Thursday | Log + Farmer + Yoke (event Cube wave) |
| Friday | Overhead Press (Cube wave, offset) + Pull-ups |

## Offset Assignment Across the 4-Cycle Program

| Cycle | Deadlift | Squat | Overhead Press |
|-------|----------|-------|----------------|
| Cycle 1, Week A | Heavy | Explosive | Volume |
| Cycle 1, Week B | Explosive | Volume | Heavy |
| Cycle 1, Week C | Volume | Heavy | Explosive |
| Cycle 2, Week A | Heavy | Explosive | Volume |
| *Pattern repeats for Cycles 3–4* | | | |

*The offset ensures maximum effort work never falls on the same day for two primary lifts.*

## Competition Event Wave (Parallel Track)

| Cycle | Event Week A | Event Week B | Event Week C |
|-------|-------------|-------------|-------------|
| Log | 90–95% singles | 60–65% × 5 explosive sets | 5 × 4 at 70% |
| Farmer | Full distance at comp weight | 4 × 20 m at 60%, start-speed focus | 4 × 30 m at 60% |
| Yoke | Full distance at comp weight | 4 × 10 m at 70%, start-speed | 3 × 25 m at 65% |
| Stones | Full series at 90%, tacky | Reduced weight, transition speed | 4–5 loads at 70%, tacky |

## Key Rules

- **Never stack heavy days.** The entire Cube system depends on the three-lift offset. If two primary lifts fall into the heavy phase on the same week, one must be shifted. The offset is not cosmetic — it is the fatigue management mechanism.
- **Explosive days are not deload days.** 8 × 2 at 60% with maximum bar speed is a CNS-intensive session. The rest interval must be 90–120 seconds minimum; if bar speed decays across sets, end the session.
- **Event heavy days use full implements at full distance.** The event heavy day is a competition simulation, not a heavy barbell session with implements. Full distance, full weight, full contest standard.

> Preceding program: `strongman_intermediate_block`. Competition-specific preparation: `strongman_competition_prep`.

# Eddie Hall Training Structure

Eddie Hall, the 2017 World's Strongest Man and holder of the first-ever 500 kg deadlift world record (2016), represents the extreme end of strongman training philosophy: **maximum intensity, maximum frequency, professional recovery infrastructure**. His training structure is not a program that intermediate or even most advanced athletes can replicate — it requires professional-grade recovery support (cryotherapy, massage therapy, sleep management, nutritional support) and is documented here as a reference for understanding the apex of the sport.

Hall's central premise was that elite strongman athletes are held back not by lack of training intensity but by inability to recover from it. His solution was to invest in recovery infrastructure equal to his training demands, allowing near-daily heavy sessions without accumulating the overreaching that limits most athletes.

The training structure is notable for two departures from conventional strongman periodization:

1. **No maintenance phases.** Events are trained at competition weight year-round, not periodized to lighter loads in the off-season.
2. **Deadlift as the primary identity lift.** The deadlift session is the anchor of every training week; all other sessions are structured around protecting recovery for the deadlift.

## Caution: Infrastructure Requirements

Hall's training structure requires:
- Professional massage therapy: 3–5 sessions per week
- Cryotherapy or ice bath access daily
- ≥ 9 hours sleep per night (documented)
- Caloric intake of 10,000+ kcal/day to support training volume at his body weight (190 kg)
- Full-time professional athlete status (no other employment)

Attempting this training volume and intensity without the recovery infrastructure produces overreaching within 4–6 weeks in most athletes.

## Who This Is For

- Professional and elite national/international strongman competitors
- Athletes with access to professional recovery support
- Athletes with 4+ years of competitive strongman experience
- Reference material for coaches designing elite-level programs

## 5-Day Weekly Schedule

| Day | Primary Focus |
|-----|---------------|
| Monday | Deadlift (heavy) + Rows |
| Tuesday | Back Squat + Overhead Press + Floor Press |
| Wednesday | Log Press (heavy) + Stones + Tire Flip + Rows |
| Thursday | Deadlift (volume/event) + Farmer Carry + Yoke |
| Friday | Squat (volume) + Overhead Press |
| Saturday | Log (max) + Stones + Accessory |

## Key Rules

- **Deadlift is non-negotiable.** Every training week contains two deadlift sessions regardless of competition proximity. Hall considered this the defining characteristic of his system.
- **Recovery is the training.** The training sessions are the stimulus; the recovery infrastructure is what makes the adaptation possible. Without equivalent recovery, reduce frequency before reducing intensity.
- **Events are never maintenance loads.** If competition weight feels manageable, it is time to increase it. Hall did not accept sub-competition loading as training stimulus at the elite level.

> This structure is not appropriate for competition prep without significant modification. For periodized competition preparation, see `strongman_competition_prep`.

# Juggernaut Method Strongman

The Juggernaut Method was developed by Chad Wesley Smith, a powerlifting world champion and professional strongman coach, as a block periodization system that provides structure and predictable progression for athletes whose linear progression has stalled. Its adaptation for strongman introduces a parallel event-training periodization track that runs alongside the barbell strength work, with both tracks converging toward competition readiness in the final block.

The defining feature of the Juggernaut Method is the **AMRAP set**: on the third week of each 4-week block, the athlete performs one all-out set to maximize reps at the prescribed weight. This serves two purposes: it generates additional training volume while providing a direct 1RM prediction that drives loading for the next block.

## Who This Is For

- Intermediate athletes whose linear progression on barbell lifts has stalled
- Athletes with at least 6–12 months of strongman training and 1–2 competitions
- Athletes with consistent access to all competition implements
- Minimum baseline: Deadlift ≥ 2× BW, Overhead Press ≥ 0.9× BW, Squat ≥ 1.75× BW

## 4-Block Structure

| Block | Weeks | Barbell Rep Target | Event Character | Deload Week |
|-------|-------|-------------------|-----------------|-------------|
| Block 1 | 1–4 | 10RM wave | Volume accumulation; technical event work | Week 4 |
| Block 2 | 5–8 | 8RM wave | Increasing event load; tacky introduced | Week 8 |
| Block 3 | 9–12 | 5RM wave | Event loads near competition; competition simulation begins | Week 12 |
| Block 4 | 13–16 | 3RM wave | Barbell volume reduced; event simulation dominant; taper | Week 16 |

## 4-Day Weekly Schedule

| Day | Focus |
|-----|-------|
| Monday | Deadlift + Squat + Posterior chain |
| Tuesday | Overhead Press + Pull-ups |
| Wednesday | Squat + Events (stones, front squat) |
| Thursday | Log + Farmer + Yoke + Pull-ups |

## Juggernaut Wave Scheme (Barbell Lifts)

| Week in Block | Sets | Reps | Load |
|---------------|------|------|------|
| Week 1 | 3 | 10/8/5/3 | 60% 1RM for target rep count + 25 kg |
| Week 2 | 3 | 10/8/5/3 | +5 kg |
| Week 3 | 3 | 10/8/5/3 + AMRAP | +5 kg (go all out on AMRAP set) |
| Week 4 | Deload | — | 50% volume; same intensity |

*Rep count in the wave header indicates the block's target (Block 1 = 10, Block 2 = 8, etc.)*

## Event Periodization by Block

| Block | Farmer Carry | Atlas Stone | Yoke | Log |
|-------|-------------|-------------|------|-----|
| Block 1 | 4 × 20 m at 50% | 3–5 loads/session, no tacky | 3 × 15 m, light | 5 × 5 at 60% |
| Block 2 | 3 × 20 m at 65% | Full series at 70%, tacky | 3 × 15 m at 70% | 4 × 3 at 75% |
| Block 3 | 3 × full distance at 80% | Full series at 85%, tacky | 2 × full distance at 85% | 3 × 2 at 85% |
| Block 4 | 2 × full distance at competition weight | Full series at competition weight | 2 × full distance at comp weight | Singles at competition weight |

## Key Rules

- **The AMRAP set is mandatory.** Never cap the reps on the AMRAP set. The final set is the most valuable data point in the entire block — it predicts your 1RM and drives the next block's loading.
- **Both tracks must progress.** If event training is stalling but barbell work is progressing, reduce the barbell block intensity temporarily and prioritize the event. The competition is decided on implements, not barbells.
- **Deload weeks are non-negotiable.** The fourth week of each block is a deload. Juggernaut's wave loading strategy depends on the deload week to allow supercompensation before the next block's loading increase.

> Preceding program: `starting_strongman`. Competition-focused continuation: `strongman_competition_prep`.

# Starting Strongman

Starting Strongman was developed by Kalle Beck of the Starting Strongman coaching organization as the definitive entry program for athletes transitioning from general barbell training into competitive strongman. Its design philosophy mirrors the "Starting Strength" model — simple linear progression on the barbell base — but integrates event training from the first week rather than treating implements as an afterthought after a strength phase.

Beck's central insight is that **amateur strongman events are learnable skills, not just strength tests**: an athlete who can deadlift 200 kg will still fail to complete an Atlas stone event without technical training, and a beginner who spends months on barbell-only work before touching a yoke will simply be an intermediate barbell athlete with poor event skills.

## Who This Is For

- Athletes with at least 6 months of barbell training experience
- Deadlift ≥ 1.5× bodyweight, Back Squat ≥ 1.25× bodyweight as minimum entry standards
- Competing in a first or second amateur competition within 12–16 weeks
- Does not require existing event experience — the program builds it

## 4-Day Weekly Schedule

| Day | Focus |
|-----|-------|
| Monday | Deadlift + Squat + Posterior chain |
| Tuesday | Overhead + Upper back |
| Wednesday | Squat + Events (stones, log) |
| Thursday | Events (farmer, yoke) + Pulls |

## Weekly Overview

| Day | Exercise | Sets × Reps | Notes |
|-----|----------|-------------|-------|
| Monday | Conventional Deadlift | 3 × 5 | Linear progression |
| | Back Squat | 5 × 5 | Linear progression |
| | Romanian Deadlift | 3 × 8 | Moderate load |
| Tuesday | Overhead Press | 4 × 5 | Strict press |
| | Push Press | 3 × 5 | After strict press |
| | Bent-Over Row | 4 × 8 | Strict form |
| Wednesday | Back Squat | 3 × 3 | Heavy |
| | Log Lift | 5 × 3 | Event-specific |
| | Atlas Stones | 3 sessions of 3–5 loads | Technique-focused |
| | Good Morning | 3 × 10 | Light/moderate |
| Thursday | Conventional Deadlift | 3 × 3 | Heavy |
| | Farmers Walk | 4 × 20 m | Straps-free |
| | Yoke Walk | 3 × 10–20 m | Light to moderate |
| | Bent-Over Row | 4 × 8 | |

## Event Progression Guidelines

### Weeks 1–4: Technical Foundation
- All implement loads are sub-maximal; technique is the only goal
- Log: focus on the clean (floor → lap → shoulder)
- Atlas stone: lap position before any loading
- Yoke: empty or minimal load; step pattern and core stability

### Weeks 5–8: Load Introduction
- Begin adding implement load systematically: 5–10% per 2 sessions
- Log: first attempts at near-competition weight at 70%
- Farmers: begin adding load when 20 m sets are comfortable
- Atlas: introduce tacky from Week 6

### Weeks 9–12: Competition Simulation
- At least one session per week at competition-standard loads
- Simulate 2 consecutive events without rest (farmer carry followed by log press) to practice event-to-event transition

## Key Rules

- **Never miss a session for grip fatigue.** Grip is the most common limiting factor at the beginner level and is a training adaptation, not a fixed trait. Train straps-free on farmers and stones. Add straps only when testing max loads.
- **Log first, barbell overhead second.** For competition-focused athletes, every overhead session should begin with the log, not the barbell. The log's center-of-mass offset and wrist position must be the primary motor pattern being reinforced.
- **Linear progression will fail — that is the signal to advance.** When the 5 kg/week deadlift increase stalls for two consecutive weeks, the athlete is ready for `strongman_intermediate_block`.

> Preceding programs: any general barbell training program. Next program: `strongman_intermediate_block`.

# Strongman Competition Preparation

Advanced strongman programming is contest-driven: the contest date defines the training structure, and the training calendar works backward from that date. The 16-week program is structured as four blocks that progress from general strength development through competition-specific practice to final peaking. Unlike powerlifting, strongman competitions vary significantly in their events — the program must be adjusted in Blocks 3–4 to reflect the specific events in the upcoming contest.

## Who This Is For

- Athletes with 2+ years of strongman training and competition experience
- Has completed `strongman_intermediate_block` or equivalent
- Competing in a specific contest on or around week 16
- Minimum baseline: Deadlift ≥ 2.5× BW, Overhead Press ≥ 1.2× BW, Back Squat ≥ 2× BW

## 16-Week Block Structure

| Block | Weeks | Character | Intensity |
|-------|-------|-----------|-----------|
| General Prep | 1–5 | Volume accumulation; all events | 70–80% |
| Specific Prep | 6–10 | Contest event emphasis; fewer events | 80–90% |
| Competition Specific | 11–14 | Contest events at contest weight | 90–100% |
| Peak/Taper | 15–16 | 50% volume; contest simulation; rest | Maintenance |

## Weekly Schedule

| Day | Focus |
|-----|-------|
| Mon | Deadlift strength + Back Squat (strength) |
| Tue | Overhead strength + Row |
| Wed | Squat volume + Front Squat |
| Thu | Event Day: Pulling (deadlift, carries, loading) |
| Fri | Event Day: Pressing + Yoke |
| Sat–Sun | Rest |

## Contest Event Integration (Blocks 3–4)

By Block 3, event training must match the specific contest implements and distances:

| Contest event type | Training simulation |
|-------------------|---------------------|
| Deadlift for reps | Max reps @ contest weight in 60 s |
| Log press for reps or max | Log press singles/reps @ contest weight |
| Farmer carry | Full distance + turn @ contest load |
| Yoke walk | Full course @ contest load |
| Atlas stone series | Stone-to-platform at each contest weight |
| Loading medley | Full medley simulation × 2 per week |

Event simulation must happen on the actual implements. If a log is not available before Block 2, acquire one before Block 3 begins.

## Competition Week Protocol

**Monday**: All contest implements at ≤50% of competition weight for single reps only. Technique confirmation — not effort work. Intensity at 50% of peak is the research consensus for the final training day before cessation (Winwood 2018).
**Tuesday–Thursday**: Rest or very light mobility work. No barbell. Complete training cessation 3–4 days before competition allows full neuromuscular restoration.
**Friday** (contest day – 1): Arrive at venue; practice warm-up routine; visualize openers.
**Saturday**: Competition.

**Attempt selection**: Open each event at 90% of best training performance, not best ever. Second attempts at 97–100%. Third attempts only for PRs when body and competition position justify the risk.

## Weight Class Management

Most strongman competitions have weight classes. If cutting:
- Stay within 5 kg of contest weight throughout the program
- Water cuts of more than 5 kg impair explosive event performance
- For same-day weigh-in: full rehydration is not possible; this is not powerlifting

See `hydration` for fluid replacement protocols. See `energy_balance` for contest prep caloric management.

> Entry point: `strongman_intermediate_block`. Preceding program: `basic_strongman_block`. Supporting knowledge: `load_management`, `deload_protocol`.

# Strongman Intermediate Block

Strongman training differs from powerlifting in two key ways: the implements change (log, axle, yoke, atlas stones replace barbell for some events), and events require sustained effort over time or distance rather than single maximum efforts. The intermediate program balances **strength base development** (barbell work) with **event training** (implement-specific sessions), distributing 2 barbell strength days and 2 event days across the week.

## Who This Is For

- Athletes who have completed `basic_strongman_block` or have 6+ months of general strength training
- Access to: barbell and plates, log (or equivalent), farmer carry handles, yoke (or substitute), atlas stones (optional — can substitute sandbag)
- Goal: first novice or masters competition, or general strength development through strongman methods

## Weekly Schedule

| Day | Session Type |
|-----|-------------|
| Monday | Strength: Lower (Deadlift + Squat) |
| Tuesday | Strength: Upper (Overhead + Row) |
| Wednesday | Rest |
| Thursday | Events: Pulling events (frame carry, deadlift medley) |
| Friday | Events: Pressing events (log press, overhead medley) |
| Sat–Sun | Rest |

## Monday — Lower Strength

| Exercise | Sets × Reps |
|----------|-------------|
| Conventional Deadlift | 5 × 2–3 (heavy) |
| Back Squat | 4 × 4–6 |
| Good Morning | 3 × 8–10 |

## Tuesday — Upper Strength

| Exercise | Sets × Reps |
|----------|-------------|
| Strict Press | 5 × 3–5 |
| Bent-over Row | 4 × 8–10 |
| Push Press | 4 × 5–8 |

## Thursday — Event Day: Pulls

Event training runs in strongman-style mini-circuits. Load each event at 70–80% of estimated maximum for technique work; push to 85–90% on the final working set:

| Event simulation | Format |
|-----------------|--------|
| Conventional Deadlift volume | 4 × 5 @ 75% |
| Farmer Carry (handles or loading equivalents) | 4 × 20 m |
| Sumo Deadlift | 3 × 5 |
| Atlas Stone to platform (or sandbag clean) | 4 × 3 reps |

When farmer handles are not available, substitute hex bar farmer carry or dumbbell farmer carry at equivalent load.

## Friday — Event Day: Press

| Event simulation | Format |
|-----------------|--------|
| Log Press (or axle press or barbell in mixed grip) | 4 × 3–5 |
| Push Press (barbell) | 4 × 5–8 |
| Yoke Walk (or front squat walk) | 4 × 20 m |
| Sandbag carry or bear hug carry | 4 × 20 m |

Event days are where the sport-specific adaptations occur. Treat them as skill training as much as strength training — implement mechanics are distinct from barbell mechanics.

## Implement Substitutions

| Standard event | Substitute if equipment unavailable |
|----------------|-------------------------------------|
| Log press | Axle bar (2" diameter) or barbell with fat grip |
| Farmer carry handles | Heavy dumbbells or hex bar |
| Yoke | Loaded barbell on traps (back squat position walk) |
| Atlas stone | Sandbag (fill to equivalent weight) |

> Entry: `basic_strongman_block`. Advanced: `strongman_competition_prep`. Injury context: `load_management`, `connective_tissue`.
